L47: ## 1. Executive Summary
L48: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L49: Anifrolumab (Saphnelo), a monoclonal antibody (mAb) that inhibits subunit 1 of the type I interferon receptor (IFNAR1), is a new biological product. The proposed indication is treatment of adults with moderate to severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) (b) (4). The proposed dosing regimen is 300 mg every 4 weeks administered as an intravenous (IV) infusion.
L50: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L51: A 351 (a) biologics license application (BLA) is submitted by AstraZeneca AB for IV administration of anifrolumab for the treatment of adult patients with moderate to severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), (b) (4) SLE is a serious and potentially life-threatening disease that can impact any organ system. This disease can cause significant morbidity and mortality as well as impact patients' function and quality of life. The toxicities associated with both the approved drugs for SLE as well as off-label treatments commonly used to treat SLE contribute to the long-term morbidity and mortality observed in patients with SLE. Only one targeted therapy, belimumab, has been approved for SLE in the last 60 years.
L52: To support the SLE indication, the Applicant provided results from two phase 3 studies, D3461C00004 (04) and D3461C00005 (05) and one phase 2 study, CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013 (1013). These studies evaluated anifrolumab IV 300 mg as well as two additional IV doses (150 mg and 1000 mg) in a total of 1,123 adults with active SLE on stable immunosuppressive background medications. From one phase 2 study (1013) and two phase 3 studies (04 &amp; 05), 459 subjects were treated with the proposed dose of 300 mg IV compared to 468 subjects treated with placebo. Patients with severe active lupus nephritis or severe active central nervous system (CNS) lupus were excluded. In addition, cyclophosphamide and the use of other biologic agents were not permitted during the studies.
L53: In all three studies, the SLE Responder Index (SRI-4) was originally selected as the primary endpoint, to establish efficacy. SRI-4 was included as the primary endpoint at week 24 in study 1013 and proposed as the primary endpoint at week 52 in study 05 and study 04. SRI-4 is a composite clinical endpoint to assess clinical response in SLE and has been successfully used to support the approval of belimumab. All three studies included the British Isles Lupus Assessment Group (BILAG)-based Composite Lupus Assessment (BICLA) endpoint. The review team notes that BICLA and SRI-4 are both considered clinically meaningful endpoints for evaluating a treatment effect on clinical response in SLE.1
L54: 1 Guidance for Industry: Systemic Lupus Erythematosus—Developing Medical Products for Treatment (June 2010); https://www.fda.gov/media/71150/download
L55: In study 1013, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (34%) achieved the primary endpoint, SRI-4 response with oral corticosteroid tapering at week 24, as compared to the placebo group (18%) [odds ratio (90% CI): 2.4 (1.3, 4.3)]. Because study 1013 was designed as a proof-of-concept and dose-ranging study and did not appropriately control for multiplicity (see section 8.1), the primary endpoint of SRI-4 is viewed as only having achieved nominal significance for benefit of anifrolumab 300 mg IV compared to placebo. However, study 1013 shared many of the features of the design and conduct of the phase 3 trials—it was randomized, placebo-controlled, 52 weeks in duration, and assessed the proposed dosing regimen in an appropriate patient population with respect to clinically relevant primary and secondary efficacy endpoints—and estimated effects were consistent with meaningful benefit. In addition to the results for the primary week 24 SRI-4 response endpoint, there was supportive evidence for SRI-4 and BICLA endpoints at week 52 (without oral corticosteroid tapering). At week 52, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (63%) achieved SRI-4 response (without oral corticosteroid tapering) as compared to the placebo group (40%) [risk difference (95% CI): 24.0 (10.9, 37.2)], and a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (54%) achieved the BICLA response (without oral corticosteroid tapering) at week 52 as compared to the placebo group (26%) [risk difference (95% CI): 28.4 (15.3, 41.5)].
L56: The first phase 3 study completed, study 05, did not show a statistically significant difference in SRI-4 response rate between anifrolumab 300 mg IV and placebo groups at week 52 [anifrolumab 36% compared to placebo 40%; risk difference (95% CI): -4.2 (-14.2, 5.8)]. Upon further review of the data, the Applicant indicated that some of the protocol-specified restrictions on medication were not implemented as intended per protocol and were not appropriate based on clinical practice. As a result, a number of subjects who would be considered responders in clinical practice were deemed non-responders due to these overly restrictive medication rules. These rules included restricted use of non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) and limited oral corticosteroids (OCCs) used for mild SLE flares. When restricted medication rules were revised in a post-hoc analysis of study 05, there was a numerically higher SRI-4 response rate observed in the anifrolumab treatment groups when compared to placebo, though not reaching nominal significance. While study 05 did not show statistically significant improvement in the prespecified primary endpoint (SRI-4), it did show nominally significant changes in the key secondary BICLA endpoint in favor of anifrolumab 300 mg IV dose. Specifically, under the revised restricted medication rules, at week 52, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (47%) achieved BICLA response as compared to the placebo group (30%) [risk difference (95% CI): 17.0 (7.2, 26.8)]. Also, disease activity measured by the SLE disease activity index (updated in 2000), (SLEDAI-2K), showed a dose-dependent separation in favor of anifrolumab in study 05.
L57: Noting the treatment effects in BICLA response rate in studies 05 and 1013, the Applicant proposed changing the primary endpoint to BICLA response rate at week 52 for the second phase 3 study (study 04) prior to database lock and unblinding. In May 2019, with agreement from the Division, the Applicant amended the protocol and statistical analysis plan for the
L58: ongoing second phase 3 trial, study 04, to change the primary endpoint from SRI-4 to BICLA response as well as to modify the restricted medication rules.
L59: Study 04 showed favorable, clinically meaningful, and statistically significant changes on the primary (BICLA) and secondary endpoints (including SRI-4) when comparing anifrolumab 300 mg IV versus placebo. Specifically, at week 52, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (48%) achieved BICLA response as compared to the placebo group (31%) [risk difference (95% CI): 16.3 (6.4, 26.3)]. Further, at week 52, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (56%) achieved SRI-4 response as compared to the placebo group (37%) [risk difference (95% CI): 18.2 (8.10, 28.3)]. The results for BICLA and SRI-4 in study 04 were consistent regardless of which medication restriction rules were applied, i.e., original versus amended.
L60: A drug's effectiveness must be established by substantial evidence2. While this has often been interpreted as requiring two adequate and well-controlled trials, there are other circumstances where substantial evidence of effectiveness can be provided outside of this setting. For example, there are situations when FDA finds one adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation with confirmatory evidence, including supportive data outside of a controlled trial, is sufficient to establish effectiveness3. In particular, when the disease is life-threatening or severely debilitating with an unmet need, additional flexibility may be warranted. The team notes that a significant unmet medical need exists for safe and efficacious treatment for patients with SLE, which was a consideration in the assessment of effectiveness in the anifrolumab application.
L61: In this case, 04 represents an adequate and well-controlled trial showing favorable and statistically significant changes on the pre-specified, clinically meaningful primary and secondary endpoints when comparing anifrolumab 300 mg IV versus placebo. Because the phase 2 trial (1013) was designed only as a proof of concept and dose-ranging study with inadequate multiplicity control and study 05, the first phase 3 trial, failed on its primary endpoint, these cannot be considered additional successful adequate and well-controlled trials when considered independently. However, the review team feels that when these trials are considered in combination, the consistent results observed in studies 1013 and 05 do provide confirmatory evidence that support the effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV.
L62: Across the three studies, there are three randomized comparisons, each with adequate duration (52 weeks), that support the claim that anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher BICLA response rates compared to placebo. Further, two of the three studies provide support that anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher SRI-4 response rates compared to placebo at week 52. While study 05 failed to show an effect on SRI-4, the study showed a numerically dose-dependent trend that is not statistically significant in SLEDAI-2K, a measure of disease activity
L63: 2 The FD&amp;C Act section 505(d) (21 U.S.C. § 355(d))
L64: 3 https://www.fda.gov/media/133660/download
L65: that is used to define both SRI-4 and BICLA and for which there was relatively consistent evidence across all three studies. In all three studies, additional supportive evidence was noted in the analyses of the secondary endpoints, including a greater proportion of subjects on anifrolumab than placebo able to reduce oral corticosteroid use and a lower proportion of subjects on anifrolumab experiencing a flare of SLE disease activity, further supporting the effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV.
L66: The current understanding of the role of Type I IFN in the pathogenesis of SLE4,5 supports the biological plausibility of targeting Type I IFN with anifrolumab in SLE and provides additional confirmatory evidence of the effectiveness of anifrolumab. In addition to the evidence provided from the clinical trials, mechanistic evidence is provided by the observed neutralization of the type I IFN gene signature in anifrolumab-treated subjects with a high baseline type I IFN gene signature in all three studies.
L67: Therefore, based on the data generated from study 04 and additional confirmatory evidence from studies 1013 and 05, and mechanistic rationale, there is substantial evidence of effectiveness of the 300 mg dosing regimen of anifrolumab IV as treatment for adult patients with active SLE who are receiving standard SLE therapy consisting of either one or any combination of oral corticosteroids (OCS), antimalarials and/or immunosuppressants at baseline.
L68: The safety of anifrolumab was evaluated in a total of 837 adult SLE patients who received IV anifrolumab (150 mg, 300 mg, or 1000 mg) during clinical trials. Of these, 688 SLE patients were exposed to anifrolumab for at least 1 year and 263 patients were exposed for at least 3 years.
L69: Integrated safety evaluation is based on two placebo-controlled datasets—one assessed through 52 weeks and one based on the 52-week controlled period and a placebo-controlled long-term extension up to three additional years. The three trials described above (1013, 05 and 04) provide data for 459 subjects treated with anifrolumab 300 compared with 466 on placebo for 52 weeks. Patients enrolled in the two phase 3 trials (05 and 04) who received placebo every 4 weeks for 52 weeks were randomized in a blinded fashion to continue on placebo or initiate treatment with anifrolumab 300 mg. Long-term comparisons including exposure beyond 52 weeks were measured for patients who were initially randomized to anifrolumab and continued on anifrolumab beyond 52 weeks, compared to patients who were initially randomized to placebo and later randomized to placebo beyond 52 weeks; in this dataset, there was greater overall exposure to anifrolumab than placebo as more patients from the anifrolumab arms than placebo arms continued beyond 52 weeks of treatment within the long-term extension study.
L70: 4 Lichtman EI, Helfgott SM, Kriegel MA. Emerging therapies for systemic lupus erythematosus— focus on targeting interferon-alpha. Clin Immuno 2012;143(3):210-21.
L71: 5 Pascual V, Farkas L, Banchereau J. Systemic lupus erythematosus: all roads lead to type I interferons. Curr Opin Immunol. 2006 Dec;18(6):676-82.
L72: Overall, the safety profile of anifrolumab IV 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in the adult SLE population that participated in studies 1013, 04, and 05 (52-week controlled dataset) appeared to be similar to placebo, with the exception of certain infections (including herpes zoster) and infusion related reactions (including two cases of angioedema). One case of anaphylaxis was reported in a subject receiving the 150 mg dose. In addition, beyond 52 weeks (phase 3 long-term extension dataset), herpes zoster and other viral infections continued to occur at a higher rate in patients treated with anifrolumab 300 mg, and fatal respiratory infections occurred only in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm, including pneumonia and COVID-19 versus placebo.
L73: In summary, the efficacy and safety data generated from studies 1013, 04, and 05, support a favorable benefit-risk assessment for approval of the 300 mg dosing regimen of anifrolumab IV as add-on treatment for adult patients with active SLE while receiving standard SLE therapy consisting of either one or any combination of oral corticosteroids (OCS), antimalarials and/or immunosuppressants at baseline.
L74: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L75: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L76: To support the proposed indication, the Applicant provided the results from three clinical studies—phase 2 study CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013 (1013) and phase 3 studies D3461C00004 (04) and D3461C00005 (05), that evaluated anifrolumab IV 300 mg as well as two additional doses (150 mg and 1000 mg) in a total of 1,123 adults with active systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) on stable immunosuppressive background medications. These were international, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 52-week studies that evaluated the efficacy, safety, and immunogenicity of the proposed dose of 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in 925 adult subjects with active SLE receiving standard therapy.
L77: Determination of substantial evidence of effectiveness in adult patients was based on efficacy results from one adequate and well-controlled study—phase 3 study (04). This was an appropriately designed, well-conducted study that adequately controlled for multiplicity, to characterize the efficacy and safety of anifrolumab in an adult population with active SLE. In study 04, anifrolumab 300 mg achieved a statistically significant and clinically meaningful higher response rate compared to placebo in the primary endpoint of BICLA at week 52, a composite of multiple measures of SLE improvement—anifrolumab 48% compared to placebo 31%, [risk difference (95% CI): 16.3 (6.3, 26.3)]. Additional supportive evidence for efficacy is provided in this study by the SRI-4 composite 52-week endpoint, where a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300mg IV subjects (56%) achieved response compared to the placebo group (37%)[risk difference (95% CI): 18.2 (8.1, 28.3)].
L78: Confirmatory evidence of effectiveness is provided by study 1013, a phase 2 study designed to provide proof of concept and dose-ranging information, and a second phase 3 study (study 05). While an appropriate method for controlling multiplicity was not pre-specified in study 1013, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (53.5%) achieved the 52-week BICLA secondary endpoint as compared to the placebo group (25.7%)[risk difference (95% CI): 28.4 (15.3, 41.5)]. Additional supportive evidence for efficacy is provided by the SRI-4 composite 52-week endpoint in study 1013, for which a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (62.6%) achieved response as compared to the placebo group (40.2%)[risk difference (95% CI):24.0 (10.9, 37.2)]. While a second phase 3 study (study 05) did not meet its primary objective and failed on the primary endpoint SRI-4, the results on BICLA and other secondary endpoints are largely consistent with effects observed in study 04 and study 1013 and are viewed as additional confirmatory evidence. In study 05, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects achieved the 52-week BICLA endpoint over placebo. This effect was consistently observed in a post-hoc analysis (using revised restricted medication rules that are more consistent with clinical practice)—a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (47%) achieved the 52-week BICLA endpoint as compared to the placebo group (30%)[risk difference (95% CI): 17.0 (7.2, 26.8)].
L79: The proposed dose of 300 mg IV demonstrated consistent benefit both for the BICLA endpoint as well as for secondary endpoints of reduction
L80: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L81: in daily steroid dose and reduction in SLE flare rate.
L82: The safety of anifrolumab 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in the SLE clinical program was consistent with expected increased risk of infection due to immunosuppression, in particular for viral infections in which Type I IFN is a central part of the immune response. While the 52-week incidence of serious infections between placebo and anifrolumab 300 mg IV arms in all three studies submitted to support approval was balanced between anifrolumab and placebo arms—anifrolumab 4.8% (22/459) vs placebo 5.6% (26/466)—the proportion of subjects with any infections was higher in subjects treated with anifrolumab 300 mg IV (69.7%; 320/459) as compared to subjects in the placebo arms (55.4%; EAIR 99.9/100 PY). Herpes zoster infections, in particular, occurred at a higher rate in anifrolumab-treated subjects compared to placebo (6.1% vs 1.3%). In the 52-week controlled study period, fatal infections occurred in 0.4% of patients receiving anifrolumab and 0.2% of patients receiving placebo. In long-term, placebo controlled assessment of subjects on anifrolumab 300 mg beyond 52 weeks, a total of seven deaths were attributed to infections in anifrolumab-treated subjects (four pneumonia and three COVID-19) as compared to none on placebo. Overall, more cases of hypersensitivity reactions and infusion-related reactions (including two cases of angioedema) occurred in subjects receiving anifrolumab compared to placebo, and one case of anaphylaxis occurred in a patient receiving the 150 mg dose.
L83: In summary, the efficacy and safety data generated from one adequate and well-controlled study—04—as well as confirmatory evidence from studies 1013 and 05, result in a favorable benefit-risk assessment for approval of the 300 mg IV every 4 weeks dosing regimen of anifrolumab as add-on treatment for adult patients with active SLE while receiving standard therapy consisting of either one or any combination of oral corticosteroids (OCS), antimalarials and/or immunosuppressants.
L84: A significant unmet medical need exists for safe and efficacious treatment for patients with SLE. In addition to the evidence provided from the clinical trials, the current understanding of the role of Type I IFN in the pathogenesis of SLE supports the biological plausibility of targeting Type I IFN with anifrolumab in SLE.
L85: If approved, anifrolumab, a first-in-class biologic with a unique mechanism of action, will provide a new treatment option for patients with SLE.
L86: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L87: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) is a chronic, multisystem, disabling autoimmune rheumatic disease of unknown etiology. Clinical manifestations of SLE can include constitutional symptoms, alopecia and rashes, serositis, inflammatory arthritis, renal disease, systemic vasculitis, lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, hemolytic anemia, cognitive dysfunction, and other central nervous system involvement. SLE affects 200,000 to 1.5 million people in the United States and at least five million people worldwide,67 with a particularly higher prevalence among racial/ethnic minority groups.8 SLE is a serious disease associated with increased morbidity and mortality with an estimate that between 10-15% of people with lupus will die prematurely due to complications of lupus.9 Uncontrolled, ongoing disease activity over time has been associated with poorer outcomes, such as organ damage and coronary artery disease in SLE.10 Higher doses of medications to control disease activity can also be harmful for many patients as they are associated with cumulative and irreversible organ damage (such as premature cataracts, retinopathy, osteoporotic fractures, cardiovascular damage, avascular necrosis, and infections).11
SLE is a serious and potentially life-threatening disease that can impact any organ system. This disease can cause significant morbidity and mortality as well as impact on patients' function and quality of life.

Aspirin, corticosteroids, hydroxychloroquine and belimumab are FDA-approved for treatment of SLE
Other therapies currently used in SLE management are used off-label
The toxicities associated with both the approved drugs for SLE as well as off-label treatments commonly used to treat SLE contribute to the long-term morbidity and mortality observed in patients with SLE.
Only one targeted therapy –belimumab–has been approved for SLE in the last 60 years.12 It targets one pathway and is therefore not effective in all patients or for all SLE disease manifestations.13
There is a significant unmet medical need for safe and efficacious treatment for adults with SLE.

Benefit	Studies CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013 (1013), D3461C00004 (04), and D3461C00005 (05) were multicenter, international, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled studies that evaluated the efficacy and safety over 52 weeks of 300 mg IV anifrolumab every 4 weeks versus placebo every 4 weeks in 925 adult subjects with active SLE receiving standard therapy.
Primary endpoint was the week 52 BICLA response rate comparing the anifrolumab IV group versus placebo group in phase 3 study 04. Primary endpoint in phase 2 study 1013 was week 24 SRI-4 plus OCS tapering. Primary endpoint in phase 3 study 05 was week 52 SRI-4.
The Applicant has submitted substantial evidence meeting the evidentiary standard for the clinical effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in subjects with SLE who are receiving standard therapy based on one adequate and well-controlled phase 3 study and confirmatory evidence from two additional studies. These studies enrolled a population of subjects who were generally representative of the targeted US patient

In study 04, a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (47.8%) achieved the 52-week BICLA primary endpoint as compared to the placebo group (31.3%)[risk difference (95% CI): 16.3 (6.3, 26.3); p=0.001].

In study 05 a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects achieved the 52-week BICLA endpoint (37.2%) as compared to the placebo group (26.6%) [risk difference (95% CI): 10.1 (0.6, 19.7)].
In a post-hoc analysis of the study 05 BICLA endpoint using the amended restricted medication rules intended to align with study 04, consistent effects were observed with a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (47.2%) achieving the 52-week BICLA endpoint as compared to the placebo group (29.9%)[risk difference (95% CI): 17.0 (7.2, 26.8)].
For its primary 52-week SRI-4 endpoint, study 05 did not demonstrate statistically significant superiority over placebo.

Lower SLE disease activity as well as a decrease in the risk for severe SLE flares may result in less long-term end-organ damage from patients' underlying disease, less treatment with unapproved immunosuppressive agents commonly used to treat SLE with their own inherent toxicities, as well as fewer hospitalizations with fewer absent work days and ultimately less morbidity and mortality associated with this disease.

Risk and Risk Management	Overall, the safety profile of anifrolumab IV assessed at a dose of 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in the adult SLE population that participated in studies 1013, 04 and 05 appeared to be similar to placebo.
Adverse events were reported in 87% of patients receiving anifrolumab 300 mg and 79% of patients receiving placebo.
The most commonly reported adverse events (\ge 5\%) during anifrolumab treatment, irrespective of causality, were nasopharyngitis, upper respiratory infection, urinary tract infection, bronchitis, infusion-related reaction, headache, herpes zoster, back pain, sinusitis, and cough.
The proportion of patients with serious adverse events was 12% for anifrolumab 300 mg and 17% for placebo.
The proportion of patients who discontinued treatment due to adverse events was 4.1% for anifrolumab 300 mg and 5.2% for placebo.
There were only 2 deaths in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV group during the 52-week study period.
In the 52-week controlled trials, fatal infections occurred in 2/459 (0.4%) of patients receiving anifrolumab and 1/466 (0.2%) of patients receiving placebo.
In the entire anifrolumab IV clinical development program, including doses of 150 mg and 1000 mg IV during a 52-week period and ongoing, long-term, phase 3 extension study of 300 mg (up to a total of 4 years), there were 11 deaths in the anifrolumab IV group and 3
The Applicant has submitted adequate data on which to conclude the initial safety of anifrolumab 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in subjects with SLE who are receiving standard therapy. Review of the safety database demonstrated that anifrolumab-treated subjects, compared to subjects treated with placebo, experienced a greater frequency of adverse events, a greater number of fatal infections, herpes zoster infections, hypersensitivity reactions including anaphylaxis, and infusion-related reactions. Although no clear risk for malignancies has been identified with long-term treatment with anifrolumab in adults, there was a numerical imbalance with more total malignancies occurring in anifrolumab-treated subjects as compared to placebo. Nonclinical data and 52-week controlled trial data support the claim of potential risk of malignancy associated with anifrolumab, and it would be prudent to continue monitoring safety data from the ongoing placebo-controlled phase 3 long-term extension study (09) for the development of

A post-marketing, required pediatric study to assess the safety, efficacy, and pharmacokinetics of anifrolumab plus background standard therapy in pediatric subjects ages 5 years to 17 years of age with active systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) will be conducted. Based on the high proportion of women of child-bearing potential among SLE patients, a post-marketing required prospective pregnancy registry to evaluate the effects of anifrolumab on pregnancy and maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes and a retrospective cohort study in a claims-based database to evaluate the effects of anifrolumab on pregnancy-related outcomes will be conducted. The pregnancy registry study may be conducted as part of a multiple-product or disease-based registry. A lactation study, milk only, in lactating women who receive anifrolumab to assess concentrations of anifrolumab in breast milk using a validated assay will be conducted. A mother-infant pair study may be required in the future depending on the results of the milk-only study.

L108: ## 2. Therapeutic Context
L109: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L110: Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) is a chronic, multisystem, disabling autoimmune rheumatic disease of unknown etiology. Clinical manifestations of SLE can include constitutional symptoms, alopecia and rashes, serositis, inflammatory arthritis, renal disease, systemic vasculitis, lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, hemolytic anemia, cognitive dysfunction, and other central nervous system involvement. SLE affects approximately 200,000 to 1.5 million people in the United States and at least five million people worldwide,14,15 with a particularly higher prevalence among racial/ethnic minority groups.16 SLE is a serious disease associated with increased morbidity and mortality with an estimate that between 10-15% of people with lupus will die prematurely due to complications of lupus.17 Uncontrolled, ongoing disease activity over time has been associated with poorer outcomes, such as organ damage and coronary artery disease in SLE.18 Higher doses of medications to control disease activity can also be harmful for many patients as they are associated with cumulative and irreversible organ damage (such as premature cataracts, retinopathy, osteoporotic fractures, cardiovascular damage, avascular necrosis, and infections).19
L111: ## Available Therapies
L112: Available therapies to treat SLE are summarized in Table 1. Prior to the recent approval of belimumab, only three other drugs were specifically approved for SLE—aspirin and oral corticosteroids (OCSs) (both circa 1950) and hydroxychloroquine in 1955. For mild disease, first line treatments include anti-malarials (hydroxychloroquine) and OCSs (e.g., prednisone). Long-term use of hydroxychloroquine can cause retinopathy and poor adherence to treatment remains an issue with this drug.20 Steroids remain a mainstay of treatment for mild to severe disease, however chronic steroid use is a contributing factor in long-term morbidity and early
L113: 14 Izmirly, P.M., Parton, H., Wang, L., McCune, W.J., Lim, S.S., Drenkard, C., Ferucci, E.D., Dall'Era, M., Gordon, C., Helmick, C.G. and Somers, E.C. (2021), Prevalence of Systemic Lupus Erythematosus in the United States: Estimates from a Meta-Analysis of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention National Lupus Registries. Arthritis &amp; Rheumatology. Accepted Author Manuscript. https://doi.org/10.1002/art.41632.
L114: 15 Pons-Estel GJ, Ugarte-Gil MF, Alarcón GS. Epidemiology of systemic lupus erythematosus. Expert Rev Clin Immunol. 2017 Aug;13(8):799-814.
L115: 16 Lim SS, Drenkard C. Epidemiology of lupus: an update. Curr Opin Rheumatol. 2015 Sep;27(5):427-32.
L116: 17 Wallace, D.J., &amp; Hahn, B.H. (2013). Dubois' lupus erythematosus and related syndromes. (8th ed.) Philadelphia, PA: Elsevier Saunders.
L117: 18 Ibañez D, Gladman DD, Urowitz MB. Adjusted mean Systemic Lupus Erythematosus Disease Activity Index-2K is a predictor of outcome in SLE. J Rheumatol. 2005 May;32(5):824-7.
L118: 19 Durcan L, Petri M. Why targeted therapies are necessary for systemic lupus erythematosus. Lupus. 2016 Sep;25(10):1070-9.
L119: 20 Fanouriakis A, Bertsias G. Changing paradigms in the treatment of systemic lupus erythematosus. Lupus Sci Med. 2019 Feb 8;6(1):e000310.
L120: cardiovascular mortality in patients with SLE.21 Additional treatment options for moderate to severe disease include immunosuppressants, such as methotrexate, azathioprine, and mycophenolate mofetil. The use of immunosuppressants is associated with an increased risk of infection, malignancy, cardiovascular disease, and bone marrow suppression, and are not effective in all patients for all manifestations of SLE.22 Only one targeted therapy has been approved for SLE in the last 60 years. Belimumab is a neutralizing anti-B-lymphocyte stimulator mAb.23 It targets one pathway, while patients are likely to have different underlying immunopathological pathways driving their SLE disease manifestations.24 As such, it is not effective in all patients. There is substantial unmet medical need in the treatment of SLE, particularly in patients with moderate or severe disease.

L131: ## 3. Regulatory Background
L132: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L133: Anifrolumab is a new molecular entity that is not currently marketed in the US or any other country. The current application is the initial BLA for anifrolumab and has been submitted to the Division of Rheumatology and Transplant Medicine for the proposed indication for the treatment of adult patients with moderate to severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), (b) (4).
L134: ## 3.2. Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L135: The Agency had multiple interactions with the Applicant regarding the anifrolumab IV clinical program. At an end-of-phase 2 meeting on September 16, 2014, the Agency provided feedback on dose selection, endpoints, primary analyses, and controlling type 1 error in the phase 3 study designs for the clinical program. On May 24, 2018, the Agency provided written responses to a type C meeting request on the overall content, format, and proposed data presentation for the planned Biologics License Application (BLA). The FDA provided additional feedback on the Integrated Summary of Efficacy (ISE), Integrated Summary of Safety (ISS), and the Integrated Summary of Immunogenicity (ISE) for this BLA on October 29, 2018. For the BLA, the Applicant proposed to provide data from a phase 2 study (1013), and two phase 3 studies (04, 05).
L136: ## Protocol Amendment 1—Change in Primary Endpoint
L137: An amended protocol and statistical analysis plan for study 04 was submitted on May 31, 2019, after both phase 3 studies were completed; however, only the data for study 05 had been unblinded and analyzed. The database for study 05 was locked for the 52-week treatment period in August 2018. The Applicant reported that the study did not meet the primary endpoint of a statistically significant reduction in disease activity, as measured by SLE Responder Index of at least 4 (SRI-4) at 52 weeks. However, the Applicant indicated that secondary endpoints that were not SRI-4-based suggested there was a potentially clinically meaningful response to anifrolumab. Therefore, the Applicant proposed to change the primary and some key secondary endpoints for study 04 prior to its database lock and unblinding. The timeline for key activities leading to changes in the primary endpoint and key secondary endpoints in study 04 are summarized in Table 2.
L138: **Table 2. Key activities leading to the changes to the primary endpoint and key secondary endpoints for study 04**
L139: Date	Activity
August 23, 2018	Study 05 database lock and unblinding
September 27, 2018	All patients in study 04 had completed 52 weeks treatment
December 28, 2018 to February 7, 2019	End of trial notifications for study 04 submitted
May 10, 2019	In conjunction with the external Steering Committee for the anifrolumab phase 3 program, a decision was made to change the primary endpoint in study 04 plus 2 of 5 key secondary endpoints
May 23, 2019	Study 04 protocol amendment to reflect changes to primary and key secondary endpoints
May 24, 2019	Study 04 SAP amendment to reflect changes to primary and key secondary endpoints
August 19, 2019	Study 04 database lock and unblinding
L140: The proposed changes to the primary and key secondary endpoints for study 04 were:
L141: - Change from initial primary endpoint of SRI-4 response at week 52 to BICLA response at week 52
- Change in key secondary endpoint from SRI-4 response at week 52 in IFN test high subgroup to BICLA response at week 52 in IFN test high subgroup
- Added key secondary endpoint of proportion of subjects with \ge 50\% reduction in number of swollen and tender joints at week 52 in subjects with \ge 6 swollen and tender joints at baseline
- SRI-4 response at week 24 demoted from key to other secondary endpoint
- No change in key secondary endpoints of proportion of subjects who achieve an OCS dose \le 7.5 gm/day at week 40 maintained through week 52; proportion of subjects with \ge 50\% reduction in CLASI activity score at week 12 in subgroup with baseline CLASI \ge 10; annualized flare rate defined by 1 or more new BILAG-2004 A or 2 or more new B items compared to previous visit
L142: The Applicant stated that the BICLA endpoint may have some advantages over the SRI-4 because (1) the 97 questions of the BILAG-2004 may assess more of the clinical diversity of the disease and (2) the BILAG-2004 requires the clinician to rate manifestations with multiple categories, rather than simply absent or present. The Applicant argues that this endpoint may be more comprehensive, give more balanced weight to affected body systems, and measure incremental improvements or worsening. The Applicant also notes that the BICLA endpoint retains the SLEDAI-2K, using it (alongside PGA) to exclude patients who worsen in ways not captured by BILAG-2004 from being classified as responders. Therefore, the Applicant proposed to replace the SRI-based endpoints in the hierarchy for study 04 with BICLA-based endpoints.
L143: ## Protocol Amendment 2—Change in Restricted Medication Rules
L144: In the phase 2 study and both phase 3 studies, patients randomized to treatment continued to receive standard of care therapy with OCSs, antimalarial agents, and/or immunosuppressants in addition to being randomized to receive either anifrolumab or placebo. The primary and several of the secondary endpoints, including all key secondary endpoints, were composite endpoints that include the condition “no use of restricted medications beyond the protocol-allowed threshold before assessment” in order for subjects to be considered responders. However, the Applicant indicated that, upon review of the unblinded data from study 05 after the week 52 database lock, some of the protocol-specified restrictions on medication were “not implemented as intended per protocol and were not appropriate based on clinical practice,” in particular regarding use of NSAIDs. A revised set of restricted medication rules, whereby every class of medication was reviewed to ensure clinical appropriateness, were applied to study data as post-hoc analyses in study 05 and were proposed to evaluate the unblinded phase 3 study (study 04). The change in restricted medication rules for NSAIDs, which had the largest impact on determination of responders versus non-responders (NR; due to taking a restricted medication), was as follows:
L145: “Patients were classified as NR for all subsequent efficacy assessments if a new NSAID was initiated and/or there was an increase in NSAID dosage after day 1.”
L146: was changed to:
L147: “Initiation of a new NSAID within 14 days of week 52 and documented use on the day before the week-52 assessment led to classification as NR at the week-52 visit. NSAID use prior to week 50 did not lead to classification as NR.”
L148: The May 2019 submission included the amended clinical study protocol and statistical analysis plan which reflect changes to the primary and key secondary endpoints as well as modifications to the restricted medication rules. The Division cautioned that the revision in the statistical plan to incorporate changes to the medication rules and primary endpoint may affect the overall interpretability of efficacy in the overall anifrolumab IV clinical program for SLE.
L149: At a pre-BLA Type B meeting with the Applicant on February 20, 2020, the following comments/recommendations were communicated to the Applicant to facilitate review of the pending BLA submission:
L150: - Provide a justification for the discrepancies in the different study results observed. Specifically, the study design and patient populations appear to be similar between Studies 04 and 05; however, the differences in the results from these two studies are difficult to reconcile.
- For each study (Study 04, 05, and 1013), present the results for BICLA and SRI-4 response rates using (a) the original medication rules, (b) the updated medication rules, and (c) ignoring adherence to medication rules or discontinuation treatment in determining response (i.e., using all observed data regardless of adherence).
- Provide an examination of the components of the SRI-4 and BICLA endpoints for each
L151: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L152: study (Study 04, 05, and 1013). It will be important to evaluate the treatment effect on the overall endpoint and each of the components of the composite outcome in supportive analyses based on all observed data collected, regardless of treatment discontinuation or protocol adherence. This will be particularly important given the uncertainty surrounding the medication rules for these studies.
L153: - Include a tipping point analysis that targets the treatment policy estimand, i.e., includes the observed data for subjects who would be considered non-responders due to treatment discontinuation or lack of adherence to restricted medication rules and consider.

L154: ## Medical Policy and Program Review Council (MPPRC) Meeting January 22, 2021
L155: The Division sought input from the MPPRC on the approvability of anifrolumab for treatment of moderate to severe SLE as well as any additional recommendations.
L156: **MPPRC Summary Recommendations:** Given the markedly positive study 04, consistency with study 1013, and supportive evidence from the post hoc analysis of study 05, the Council deemed the evidence for effectiveness persuasive. In general, the Council considered two approaches, first considering 1013 an AWC (adequate and well-controlled) study, if sufficiently justified from statistical perspective, and study 04 as the second AWC study providing substantial evidence. Further discussions regarding the multiplicity control in 1013 were recommended. The second approach would be to consider 04 as the single AWC trial, and the other clinical data as providing confirmatory evidence.
L157: ## 4. Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L158: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L159: Clinical data from three studies (D3461C0004, D3461C00005, and CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013) were submitted to the Agency in support of a biologics licensing application (BLA) 761123 for anifrolumab to treat adult patients with moderate to severe, auto-antibody positive systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) (b)(4). Clinical inspections of the Applicant AstraZeneca Pharmaceuticals L.P., and four clinical investigators (Dr. Anurekha Chadha, site 7904, Dr. Sabeen Najam, site 7834, Dr. Phillip Waller, site 7815, and Dr. Eric Lee, site 7825) were conducted in support of this application.
L160: Maintenance of blinding was adequate throughout for all three studies (DC346C00004, DC346100005, CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013.) The data and safety monitoring board (DSMB) consisted of multi-disciplinary group of two rheumatologists, one infectious disease, one nephrologist, and one biostatistician. On 1/25/2019, DSMB minutes document how study D3461C00005 did not meet the primary efficacy endpoint. Study D3461C00005 was closed out and high level, aggregate efficacy and safety data were presented to the full DSMB, (b)(4) and (b)(4) attendees. The Applicant and (b)(4) medical team remained blinded to study D3461C00004. The DSMB reviewed the Closed Report (partially unblinded by masked treatment arms) for study D3461C00004 during the closed session.
L161: In general, the Applicant appeared to be in compliance with Good Clinical Practices. A Form FDA 483 (Inspectional Observations) was not issued. Data submitted by this Applicant appear acceptable in support of this biologic license application.
L162: Based on the results of the clinical investigators and Applicant inspections, Protocols D3461C0004, D3461C00005, and CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013 appear to have been conducted adequately, and the data generated appear acceptable in support of the proposed indication. Of note, the OSI review team identified issues with the data generated from two sites, Dr. Sabeen Najam and Dr. Phillip Waller, because of failure to prepare and maintain adequate and accurate source records for study D3461C00004. However, this was limited to the two sites and was considered related to the use of site-specific software, which was used only by these two sites. No data integrity issues were identified at Anurekha Chadha and Dr. Eric Lee sites. A sensitivity analysis conducted by our statistical reviewers confirms that the exclusion of the data generated from Dr. Sabeen Najam and Dr. Phillip Waller sites does not impact the overall efficacy conclusions.
L163: Overall, based on completed OSI inspections as well as robustness of the efficacy data, the conclusions on efficacy to support approval are reliable.
L164: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L165: The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ), CDER, recommends approval of STN 761123 for anifrolumab manufactured by AstraZeneca. The data submitted in this application are adequate to support the conclusion that the manufacture of anifrolumab is well-controlled and leads to a product that is pure and potent. It is recommended that this product be approved for human use under conditions specified in the package insert.
L166: Glycosylation analysis of anifrolumab showed that it contains [REDACTED](b)(4)% galactose-alpha-1,3-galactose (alpha-gal) on the product Fc domain. Alpha-gal on proteins is a known allergen that may produce anti-alpha-gal IgE mediated anaphylactic and hypersensitivity reaction. Detail analysis of the clinical data, especially the anaphylaxis and hypersensitivity cases, demonstrated that risk of alpha-gal associated hypersensitivity and anaphylaxis for SAPHNELO was low (see clinical section for detail).
L167: The product quality postmarketing commitment between OPQ and the Applicant listed below should be included in the action letter:
L168: Implement [REDACTED](b)(4) of anifrolumab drug product and update module 3.2.P.3.4 of the BLA with [REDACTED](b)(4) as a Critical Process Parameter.
Final report submission: 9/2023

L173: ## 5. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L174: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L175: The Applicant has conducted a comprehensive program of pharmacology, pharmacokinetic (PK), and toxicology studies with anifrolumab to support clinical development and the BLA submission. All studies submitted to support approval have been reviewed previously under IND 101849. The key findings from the nonclinical studies are cited and summarized in this review to support the approval of BLA 761123. The Applicant has also submitted extractables and leachables studies and safety assessments, which have been reviewed under this BLA (see Section 16.3). The safety assessment of impurities from the drug substance and drug product is reviewed in Section 16.3.
L176: Pharmacology studies demonstrated that anifrolumab bound to IFNAR1 and inhibited the expression of Type-I IFN responsive genes in peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) and the expression of differentiation markers and cytokines/chemokines from monocytes. An anifrolumab mouse surrogate was effective in a murine model of scleroderma and an accelerated NZB/W F1 murine model of lupus.
L177: Anifrolumab had no or reduced binding to Fc Gamma Receptors I (FcγRI), FcγRIIA, FcγRIIB, or FcγRIIIA in vitro. No complement dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) or antibody dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC) were noted in vitro. In vitro anifrolumab-positive human and cynomolgus monkey tissue binding was consistent with published reports of broad expression of IFNAR in the corresponding tissues.
L178: The effect of anifrolumab on the cardiovascular (CV) system was evaluated in a GLP-compliant 39-week general IV toxicity study in monkeys (Study# 7140-129). In this study, cynomolgus monkeys were given 40 IV infusion (30 minutes) doses of anifrolumab at 5 and 50 mg/kg/week once a week (QW) or 40 subcutaneous (SC) injection doses of anifrolumab at 15 and 60 mg/kg/week QW. Electrocardiography (ECG) parameters and blood pressure (BP) parameters were measured once during the predose phase, on Days 3 and 276 of the dosing phase, and on Day 83 of the recovery phase for these animals after they were anesthetized with ketamine. No drug-related adverse CV findings were noted. In another 4-week IV toxicity study in cynomolgus monkeys with lower systemic exposures (see Section 5.4), ECG parameters and BP were measured within 1 hr. post dosing on Day 22 (weekly dose, Study# (b)(4) 263.01). No drug-related adverse CV findings were noted.
L179: GLP-compliant toxicology studies up to 39 weeks indicated that anifrolumab was well-tolerated in cynomolgus monkeys. No drug-related adverse clinical sign changes were noted. No drug-related CV toxicity was noted. Besides anti-drug antibody (ADA)-induced minimal arteritis in multiple organs in a few male animals given 40 doses of anifrolumab at 50 mg/kg/week (IV) or 60 mg/kg/week (SC), no additional drug-related adverse effect was noted. Further, anifrolumab
L180: was not found to induce reproductive and developmental toxicity. In the GLP-compliant 39-week toxicity study in sexually mature cynomolgus monkeys, no drug-related effects on reproductive organs/tissues, semen analysis, spermatogenesis analysis, or menstrual cycle were present. In the GLP-compliant enhanced prenatal and postnatal development (ePPND) study in pregnant cynomolgus monkeys, no drug-related adverse effects in maternal animals or infants (body growth, behavior, functional, morphological, and skeletal development, and T-cell-dependent antibody response) were noted. The plasma exposures of anifrolumab at the no-observed-adverse-effect levels (NOAELs) for general toxicity and reproductive and developmental toxicity are &gt;59- and 28-fold higher than the human exposure (AUC) at the MRHD.
L181: Genetic toxicity studies have not been conducted with anifrolumab as they are generally not applicable for biotechnology-derived pharmaceuticals. Carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with anifrolumab, however the existing literature from studies with IFNAR1- and IFNAR2-deficient mice and mice administered IFN \alpha/\beta and anti-IFN-\alpha/\beta antibodies suggested that IFNAR blockade increased carcinogenic potential in rodents. The Applicant and review team have agreed on proposed language to describe this finding based on the pharmacology of anifrolumab in the product labeling.
L182: ## 5.2. Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L183: None
L184: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L185: Multiple in vitro pharmacology studies have been conducted identifying the mechanism of action and activity of anifrolumab. None of the primary or secondary pharmacology studies were conducted in compliance with GLP. In the opinion of the nonclinical reviewer, this is a typical approach in drug research and does not affect the integrity of the application.
L186: ## Primary pharmacology:
L187: Anifrolumab is a first-in-class product targeting IFNAR1. An Established Pharmacologic Class (EPC) assessment was conducted. Based on following studies, anifrolumab is considered a Type I Interferon Receptor Antagonist.
L188: - Anifrolumab has high affinity for IFNAR1 expressed on human PBMCs with KD of 0.29 nM (Study# RIA546-0002);
- Anifrolumab bound to the sub-domain 3 of human IFNAR1 (265-307) with residues 276-279 being identified as the critical epitope (Study# RIA546-0004);
- Anifrolumab competitively inhibited the binding of IFN-\alpha-2a to IFNAR in Daudi cells (Study# RIA546-0003);
- Anifrolumab inhibitedIFN-stimulated response elements (ISRE) activation induced by all the recombinant human IFN-\alpha subtypes (Study# RIA546-0006) and SLE sera (Study# RIA546-0008);
L189: - the expression of Type-I IFN responsive genes (*USP18*, *HERC5/6*, *IFIT1/3/5*, *G1P2/3*, *MX1/2*, *OAS1/2*, *IFI44*, *IFI44L*, *Ly6E*) from PBMCs by sera of SLE patients (Study# RIA546-0005);
- the expression of differentiation markers (CD123 and CD38) and cytokines/chemokines (IL-6, IL-10, MCP-1, MCP-2) from monocytes by leukocyte-IFNs (Study# RIA546-0005);
- Anti-murine IFNAR1 MAb 5A3 (anifrolumab surrogate) was effective in a murine model of scleroderma (Study# RIA546-0007) and an accelerated NZB/W F1 murine model of lupus (Study# RIA546-0011).
L190: In cross-species studies, anifrolumab was found to
L191: - have high affinity for IFNAR1 expressed on cynomolgus monkey PBMCs with KD of 0.65 nM (Study# RIA546-0002);
- inhibit ISRE activation induced by most cynomolgus recombinant type I IFNs with IC50 values comparable to the corresponding human subtypes with the exception of the IFN-\alpha1, IFN-\alpha4, and IFN-\alpha6 subtypes (Study# RIA546-0006);
- have no effect on murine IFN-\alpha-mediated ISRE activation in cells expressing murine IFNAR1 (Study# RIA546-0006).
L192: ## Secondary pharmacology:
L193: - Anifrolumab had no or significantly reduced binding to Fc\gammaR1 and Fc\gammaRIIIA in vitro (Study# RIA546-0001);
- Anifrolumab had no binding to Fc\gammaRIIA or Fc\gammaRIIB in vitro (Study# RIA546-0010);
- Anifrolumab bound to Fc Gamma Neonatal Receptor (FcRn) with KD similar to wild type in vitro, suggesting the normal IgG recycling property (Study# RIA546-0010);
- Anifrolumab did not induce CDC in human CD20+ IFNAR1+ target Daudi B cells (Study# RIA546-0009);
- Anifrolumab did not induce ADCC in IFNAR expressing 293F cells (Study# RIA546-0001 and RIA546-0012).
L194: ## Safety Pharmacology
L195: No stand-alone safety pharmacology studies were conducted with anifrolumab. The effect of anifrolumab on the CV system was evaluated in a GLP-compliant 39-week general IV toxicity study in monkeys (Study# 7140-129). In this study, cynomolgus monkeys were given 40 IV infusion (30 minutes) doses of anifrolumab at 5 and 50 mg/kg/week QW or 40 SC injection doses of anifrolumab at 15 and 60 mg/kg/week QW. ECG parameters and BP parameters were measured once during the predose phase, on Days 3 and 276 of the dosing phase, and on Day 83 of the recovery phase for these animals after they were anesthetized with ketamine. No drug-related adverse CV findings were noted. In another 4-week IV toxicity study in cynomolgus monkeys with lower systemic exposures (see Section 5.4), ECG parameters and BP were measured within 1 hr. post dosing on Day 22 (weekly dose, Study# 263.01). No drug-related adverse CV findings were noted.
L196: The effect of anifrolumab on the central nervous system or respiratory system was not evaluated. However, no related clinical sign changes were noted in general toxicity studies described in Section 5.5.1.

L205: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L206: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L207: General toxicity studies with anifrolumab were conducted in cynomolgus monkeys following single and repeated IV and SC administration with dosing durations up to 39 weeks. Cynomolgus monkey was selected as the pharmacologically relevant species based on comparable binding affinity and neutralization ability in cynomolgus monkey cells (COS-7) but not murine cells (MLE-12). All of the pivotal GLP-compliant studies were reviewed previously under IND 101849 (DARRTS Reference ID: 2892411 and 4763101). Key findings for the 39-week repeat-dose study are summarized below. Since no additional findings were noted in shorter-term toxicity studies, results from single dose and 4-week repeat-dose studies are not included in the table below.
L208: ## 39-week Intravenous Infusion Repeat-Dose Toxicity Study in Monkeys
L209: **Study title/ number:** 39-week Repeat Subcutaneous Injection and Intravenous Infusion Toxicity, Toxicokinetic, and Immunogenicity Study with MEDI-546 in Cynomolgus Monkeys with a 12-week Recovery Period (Study# 7140-129)
L210: ## Key Study Findings
L211: - Minimal arteritis was noted in 4 males given 50 mg/kg via IV infusion or 60 mg/kg via SC injection with less severity in recovery animals. Heart, kidney, and pancreas were the major organs affected. The incidence of arteritis was likely due to ADA-mediated formation of immunocomplexes.

Females: 50 mg/kg/dose IV and 60 mg/kg/dose SC
L235: ## General toxicology; additional studies
L236: In a GLP-compliant single-dose toxicity study, cynomolgus monkeys were given a single IV infusion (30 minutes) dose of anifrolumab at 0.003, 0.03, 0.3, and 1 mg/kg (Study# 7140-131). No drug-related toxicity was noted. ADA was detected in all dose groups with the highest antibody titers occurring on Day 29 at 1 mg/kg. The NOAEL was 1 mg/kg (AUC_{0-t} = 75.8 \mu g \cdot d/mL).
L237: In a GLP-compliant 4-week repeat-dose toxicity study with a 7-week recovery period, cynomolgus monkeys were given 4 IV infusion (30 minutes) doses of anifrolumab at 0.5, 5, and 30 mg/kg/week QW (Study# 263.01). The only toxicity noted was a transient (up to 4 days during the first 9 days) finding of dry feces in a dose-related manner. No hypersensitivity reaction was observed. ADA was detected in all dose groups, but the percentage of monkeys with positive titers decreased with increasing anifrolumab doses. The NOAEL was 30 mg/kg (AUC_{0-7d} = 11,100 \mu g \cdot d/mL).
L238: ## 5.5.2. Genetic Toxicology
L239: Genetic toxicity studies have not been conducted with anifrolumab. Genetic toxicity studies are generally not applicable for biotechnology-derived pharmaceuticals such as anifrolumab, as discussed in the ICH S6(R1) guidance document.
L240: ## 5.5.3. Carcinogenicity
L241: Carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with anifrolumab. However, the existing literature from studies with IFNAR1- and IFNAR2-deficient mice and mice administered IFN \alpha/\beta and anti-IFN-\alpha/\beta antibodies suggested that IFNAR blockade increased carcinogenic potential in rodents.

L266: ## 6. Clinical Pharmacology
L267: ## 6.1. Executive Summary
L268: Anifrolumab is a monoclonal antibody (mAb) that inhibits subunit 1 of the type I interferon receptor (IFNAR1). AstraZeneca submitted BLA 761123 on July 31, 2020 seeking the marketing approval for anifrolumab for the treatment of adults with moderate to severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) (b)(4). The proposed dosing regimen is 300 mg every 4 weeks administered as an intravenous (IV) infusion.
L269: BLA 761123 consists of eight clinical and clinical pharmacology studies, including two phase 1 studies, three phase 2 studies, and three phase 3 studies. In addition, three reports for population PK analysis, exposure-response modeling, and integrated summary of immunogenicity were submitted.
L270: The following are the major clinical pharmacology findings of the current review:
L271: - Anifrolumab exhibits non-linear PK in the dose range of 100 mg to 1000 mg with AUC increased more than dose-proportionally due to IFNAR1-mediated clearance. Following the every 4 weeks IV administrations of anifrolumab, steady-state was reached by Day 85. The steady state exposure (AUC) for 1000 mg q4w is approximately 4 fold higher compared to the steady state exposure of 300 mg q4w. The steady state exposure (AUC) for 300 mg q4w is approximately 3 fold higher compared to the steady state exposure of 150 mg q4w.
- The dosing regimen of anifrolumab has been adequately explored. Results from phase 2 dose-ranging study (1013) showed that while both 300 mg and 1000 mg q4w dosing regimens were efficacious in SLE patients, 1000 mg dosing did not provide additional treatment benefit. Therefore, IV 300 mg q4w dosing regimen was selected for phase 3 studies (04 and 05). A 150 mg dose group was included in the Phase 3 program to further elucidate dose response. The responder rate was numerically lower at 150 mg q4w. Exposure-response analysis for efficacy and safety indicated that the proposed IV 300 mg q4w dosing regimen provides the optimal benefit-risk balance in patients with SLE.
- No dose adjustment of anifrolumab is recommended for any intrinsic or extrinsic factors.
- In Phase 3 studies 04 and 05, 6 out of 352 subjects (1.7%) with anifrolumab IV 300 mg q4w treatment were detected ADA positive post baseline. Due to the limited number of subjects, no conclusion could be drawn regarding the impact of positive ADA on anifrolumab PK, PD, efficacy, and safety.
- In patients with SLE, following the administration of anifrolumab at 300 mg IV q4w for 52 weeks, neutralization (\ge 80\%) of type I IFN gene signature was observed from week 4 to week 52 in blood samples of patients with elevated levels of type I IFN inducible
L272: genes. This PD marker returned to baseline levels within 8 to 12 weeks following withdrawal of anifrolumab at the end of the 52-week treatment period. However, the clinical relevance of the type I IFN gene signature neutralization is unclear.
L273: - In SLE patients with positive anti-dsDNA antibodies at baseline (Studies 04 and 05), treatment with anifrolumab 300 mg led to numerical reductions in anti-dsDNA antibodies over time through week 52.
- In patients with low complement levels (C3 and C4), increases in complement levels were observed in patients receiving anifrolumab through week 52.
L274: ## 6.2. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L275: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology, Division of Inflammation and Immune Pharmacology (DIIP) and Division of Pharmacometrics (DPM) have reviewed the clinical pharmacology data submitted under BLA 761123. This submission is recommended for approval from a clinical pharmacology perspective for the treatment of patients with SLE.
L276: ## 6.2.1. Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L277: Anifrolumab is a mAb that inhibits IFNAR1. It exhibits non-linear PK in the dose range of 100 mg to 1000 mg due to IFNAR1-mediated clearance. Population PK analysis showed baseline type I IFN gene signature status and body weight have a significant impact on anifrolumab CL, but were not considered clinically relevant.
L278: ## 6.2.2. General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L279: ## General Dosing
L280: The proposed dosing regimen is 300 mg every 4 weeks administered as an IV infusion.
L281: ## Therapeutic Individualization
L282: None
L283: ## Outstanding Issues
L284: None
L285: ## 6.3. Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
L286: ## 6.3.1. General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L287: Anifrolumab is a mAb that inhibits IFNAR1. Based on population PK analysis, anifrolumab exhibits non-linear PK in the dose range of 100 mg to 1000 mg with AUC increased more than
L288: dose-proportionally due to IFNAR1-mediated clearance and the estimated clearance for anifrolumab is 0.193 L/day (Figure 1). Following every 4 weeks IV administrations of anifrolumab, steady-state was reached by Day 85 (Figure 2). There was minimal accumulation in Cmax after multiple dosing with an accumulation ratio of 1.36 at the 300 mg dose level and 1.43 at the 1000 mg dose level (Table 3). Mean Ctrough doubled after multiple doses with an accumulation ratio of 2.49 at the 300 mg dose level and 2.29 at the 1000 mg dose level (Figure 2, Table 3). Based on population pharmacokinetics, the steady state exposure (AUC) for 1000 mg q4w is approximately 4 fold higher compared to the steady state exposure of 300 mg q4w. The steady state exposure (AUC) for 300 mg q4w is approximately 3 fold higher compared to the steady state exposure of 150 mg q4w.

L304: ## 6.3.2. Clinical Pharmacology Questions
L305: ## Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?
L306: This submission consists of 8 clinical and clinical pharmacology studies, including 2 phase 1 studies, 3 phase 2 studies, and 3 phase 3 clinical studies (Table 4). The proposed indication of anifrolumab was based on the three studies submitted to support approval (phase 2 study 1013 and phase 3 studies 04 and 05 (see section 7 and 8). The PD assessment of anifrolumab, i.e. the 21-gene type I IFN signature, provided supportive evidence of effectiveness.
L307: ## What is the PD effect of Anifrolumab?
L308: ## Type 1 IFN gene signature
L309: Anifrolumab is a human IgG1k mAb that binds to IFNAR1 and inhibits type I IFN signaling by blocking the biologic activity of type I IFNs. Type I IFN signaling leads to the transcription activation and expression of over 100 genes, of which a subset of 21 genes was shown to be robustly upregulated by type I IFNs and used to monitor the impact of IFNAR blockade in blood. The 21-gene type I IFN signature was assessed in clinical studies as a PD biomarker of anifrolumab activity.
L310: The SLE patient population has been stratified based on a 4-gene type I IFN gene signature test, where the genes in the subset were the most highly expressed in the target SLE patient population and the test results (high or low) were based on the over-expression of type I IFN-inducible genes. Approximately 75% patients in study 1013 and 82-83% patients in Studies 04 and 05 were type I IFN gene signature test high at baseline.
L311: In the pooled PD analysis, 300 mg and 1000 mg resulted in \ge 80\% median neutralization of the type I IFN gene signature by week 4 and attained over 52-week treatment period in patients with high type I IFN gene signature, whereas a much less extent of PD neutralization was observed in the 150 mg group (Figure 3). While neutralization of the type I IFN gene signature reflects the direct pharmacological effect of anifrolumab and there is a clear dose-dependent PD response, its clinical relevance is still unclear as the PD response is higher in the non-responders on the 300 mg arm compared to the responders on the 150 mg arm (Figure 4).

L324: ## Immunology/serology (anti-dsDNA antibodies, C3, C4)
L325: In the pooled phase 3 studies (Studies 04 and 05), for SLE patients with positive anti-dsDNA antibodies at baseline (median levels of anti-dsDNA were 50.1 U/mL and 53.0 U/mL, in the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups, respectively), treatment with anifrolumab 300 mg led to numerical reductions in anti-dsDNA antibody levels over time through week 52 compared with the placebo group (median change from baseline of -14.82 U/mL vs -5.37 U/mL at week 52).
L326: In the pooled phase 3 studies (Studies 04 and 05), the mean levels of C3 at baseline were 0.691 g/L in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 0.701 g/L in the placebo group. For patients with an abnormal C3 level at baseline, the anifrolumab 300 mg group had greater numerical increases in C3 levels over the 52 weeks compared with the placebo group (mean change from baseline of 0.13 g/L vs 0.04 g/L at week 52). The mean levels of C4 at baseline were 0.073 g/L in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 0.072 g/L in the placebo group. In patients with low/abnormal complement levels at baseline, normalization of C4 was observed at week 52 in 22.6% (n=19) of patients receiving anifrolumab and in 7.1% (n=6) of patients receiving placebo.
L327: ## **Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for which the indication is being sought?**
L328: The proposed dosing regimen, IV 300 mg q4w, is appropriate for the general patient population for the treatment of patients with SLE.
L329: ## **Phase 2 dose-ranging study**
L330: Study 1013 is a phase 2, multinational, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of 2 IV dosing regimens in subjects with chronic, moderately-to-severely active SLE with an inadequate response to SOC SLE. A total of approximately 300 subjects were randomized (1:1:1) to receive IV dose of anifrolumab 300 or 1000 mg or placebo q4w for 48 weeks. Results indicated that both 300 mg and 1000 mg treatment groups showed statistical improvement in the primary endpoint, SRI(4) with OCS tapering at week 24, over placebo and the response rates were similar between 300 mg and 1000 mg (34.3% and 28.8%, respectively, versus 17.6% in placebo). Thus, 300 mg q4w was considered appropriate for the Phase 3 program.
L331: As 300 mg and 1000 mg appear to be on the plateau part of the dose-response curve for efficacy, a 150 mg dose group was included in the Phase 3 program to elucidate dose response and provide additional data to characterize benefit risk.
L332: Figure 5. SRI(4) response rate without OCS tapering requirement (study 1013)
L333: (Source: Figure 11.4.1.1-1 of Study 1013 CSR)
L334: ## Exposure-response for efficacy and safety
L335: The Applicant conducted exposure response (ER) analysis based on the two phase 3 studies with two dosing regimens (300 mg q4w, 150 mg q4w). The exposure response relationships for efficacy endpoints (BICLA and SRI(4)) and the safety event of interest (herpes zoster) are depicted in Figure 6 and Figure 7 below. For details in ER analysis, see section 16.4.3 Exposure Response Relationship.
L336: Figure 6. Exposure response (BICLA) relationship in patients with type I IFN gene signature test high who completed treatment
L337: Exposure response for SRI(4) by treatment (placebo, 150 mg, 300 mg)
L338: Exposure response for SRI(4) by exposure based quartiles of 300 mg
L339: Exposure response for herpes zoster by treatment (placebo, 150 mg, 300 mg)
L340: (Source: reviewer's analysis Figure 33)
L341: Figure 7. Exposure response (SRI(4)) relationship in patients who completed treatment
L342: Exposure response for SRI(4) by treatment (placebo, 150 mg, 300 mg)
L343: Exposure response for SRI(4) by exposure based quartiles of 300 mg
L344: Exposure response for herpes zoster by treatment (placebo, 150 mg, 300 mg)
L345: (Source: reviewer's analysis Figure 37)
L346: For the exposure-response for efficacy, for both BICLA and SRI(4), both responder rates were at its maximal levels at 300 mg q4w without further improvement at 1000 mg q4w, and numerically lower at 150 mg q4w. For the exposure-response for safety, the observed herpes zoster incidence rates in the Phase 2 study were 2.0%, 5.1%, and 9.5% for placebo, 300 mg q4w, and 1000 mg q4w, respectively. This result was consistent with the incidence rate observed in the Phase 3 studies, and together supported a dose dependent herpes zoster incidence rate from 150 mg to 1000 mg. These efficacy and safety ER analyses supported the 300 mg q4w dosing regimen as the optimized dosing regimen.
L347: ## Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors?
L348: No dose adjustment is needed for subpopulations based on intrinsic factors.
L349: In population PK analysis, body weight and baseline type I IFN status were identified as significant covariates. Patients with higher body weight and type I IFN test high patients were found to have higher CL, however, the difference was not considered clinically relevant.
L350: Patients with high UPCR appeared to have higher CL, but the impact on PK was not significant. The exclusion criteria for UPCR was 2 mg/mg (or 226.30 mg/mmol) in the SLE clinical trials. Other intrinsic factors such as race/ethnicity/region, age, sex, and renal/hepatic function tests have no impact on the PK. See section 16.4 for details.
L351: ## **Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is the appropriate management strategy?**
L352: Anifrolumab is a mAb and proposed to be administered through IV infusion. There are no clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions.
L353: Based on population PK analysis, concomitant use of oral corticosteroids, anti-malarials, immunosuppressants (azathioprine, methotrexate, mycophenolate mofetil, mycophenolic acid, and mizoribine), NSAIDs, ACE inhibitors, and HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors did not significantly affect the PK of anifrolumab.
L354: ## **What is the incidence of ADA? What is the impact of ADA on the PK, PD, safety, and clinical outcomes of the biological product (e.g., therapeutic protein)?**
L355: In phase 3 studies 04 and 05, 6 out of 352 subjects (1.7%) with anifrolumab IV 300 mg q4w treatment and 17 out of 357 subjects (4.8%) with placebo were detected ADA positive post baseline. Due to the limited number of subjects, no conclusion could be drawn regarding the impact of positive ADA on anifrolumab PK, PD, efficacy, and safety.
L356: Refer to the OBP review regarding the bioanalytical assay for immunogenicity assessment.

L381: ## 7.2. Review Strategy
L382: The clinical review program supporting registration of anifrolumab for the treatment of SLE consisted of 1 Phase 2 trial (CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013) and 2 Phase 3 trials (D3461C00004 and D3461C00005). See section 8.3 for a detailed description of these studies.
L383: No pooling of data was performed for the efficacy analysis. For the evaluation of safety, the 52-week safety data for the 300 mg IV dose was summarized in the clinical study reports for studies 1013, 04 and 05.
L384: The application contained double-blind safety data from adult SLE patients collected over 52 weeks of 300 mg IV anifrolumab dosing in studies 1013, 04 and 05. Safety data for all 3 doses of anifrolumab (150 mg, 300 mg, and 1000 mg) from studies 1013, 04 and 05 plus study 1145 (open-label extension from study 1013) and for the 300 mg dose in study 09 (placebo-controlled long-term extension from studies 04 and 05) were also included for completeness.
L385: ## 8. Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation
L386: ## 8.1. Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L387: ## 8.1.1. CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013 (Phase 2), D3461C00004 (Phase 3), and D3461C00005 (Phase 3)
L388: ## Trial Designs
L389: ## Study 1013
L390: Study CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013 (1013) was a 52-week, multi-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group study. Patients were randomized in a 1:1:1 fashion to MEDI-546 (anifrolumab) 1000mg, MEDI-546 300mg, or placebo (Figure 8). Anifrolumab or placebo was administered as an IV infusion via an infusion pump for approximately 60 minutes every 4 weeks for a total of 13 doses (first dose at Day 1 (week 0) and last dose at Day 337 (week 48)). All patients were to be followed for 85 days after Day 337 through Day 422 (week 60). Randomization was stratified by (SLEDAI-2K) score at screening (&lt; 10 points vs \ge 10 points), Day 1 OCS dose (&lt; 10 mg/day vs \ge 10 mg/day of prednisone or equivalent), and the results of a diagnostic test for type I IFN signature in whole blood at screening using a 4-gene type I IFN test (positive vs negative).
L391: The study design included oral corticosteroid (OCS) tapering targets. Patients who experienced an increase in SLE activity were permitted one burst of OCS or intramuscular corticosteroids between Day 1 and 71, however, were required to taper to Day 1 levels or less within 14 days and to complete by Day 71, otherwise they would be declared non-responders for the primary and disease activity related secondary endpoints. Further, patients who required an increase of OCS above Day 1 dose levels after Day 71 were to be declared non-responders for the primary endpoint and disease activity related secondary endpoints at Day 169 (week 24). In addition, patients who were unable to reduce OCS to &lt; 10 mg/day and \le Day 1 dose of prednisone or equivalent by Day 85 and unable to maintain OCS doses &lt; 10 mg/day and \le Day 1 dose of prednisone or equivalent until Day 169 were to be declared non-responders for the primary endpoint at Day 169 (week 24).
L392: Patients were also permitted to receive one additional burst of OCS or intramuscular corticosteroids between Days 169 and 281 and were not to be declared a non-responder for disease activity secondary endpoints at Day 365 (week 52) if they tapered to Day 169 levels or less within 14 days and completed by Day 281. Patients who required an increase of OCS above the minimum of Day 1 dose or Day 169 dose level after Day 281 were to be declared non-responders for disease activity secondary endpoints at Day 365 (week 52). Patients who were unable to reduce OCS to &lt; 10 mg/day and \le Day 1 dose of prednisone or equivalent by Day 281 and unable to maintain OCS doses &lt; 10 mg/day and \le Day 1 dose until Day 365 were to be declared non-responders for certain disease activity secondary endpoints at Day 365 (week 52).
L393: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L394: Investigators should have tapered OCS based on disease activity in all subjects throughout the study, as permitted between Days 1 and 113 and between Days 169 and 309. Patients who permanently discontinued from receiving investigational product were to be followed for safety. Further, two follow-up visits were scheduled for Day 396 (week 56) and Day 422 (week 60).
L395: Key inclusion criteria include:
L396: - 18 to 65 years of age
- Fulfilled at least 4 of the 11 American College of Rheumatology (ACR) classification criteria for SLE
- Diagnosis of pediatric or adult SLE with chronic disease activity requiring ongoing treatment or observation for \ge 24 weeks prior to screening
- Prior to Day 1, external adjudication group confirmation of both:At screening, SLEDAI-2K score \ge 6 points and “Clinical” SLEDAI-2K score \ge 4 points:
- At least one of the following:BILAG-2004 Index A disease in \ge 1 body/organ system
- BILAG-2004 Index B disease in \ge 2 body/organ systems
- Day 1 “Clinical” SLEDAI-2K score \ge 4 points
- Physician Global Assessment (MDGA) \ge 1.0 on a 0 to 3 Visual Analog Scale (VAS) scale at screening
L397: Key exclusion criteria included:
L398: - Receipt of any of the following:Any new oral prednisone therapy (or equivalent) anytime from 2 weeks prior to signing of the informed consent through Day 1 or any change in current oral prednisone dose (or equivalent) anytime from 2 weeks prior to Day 1
- Any new dose of any of the following anytime in the 12 weeks prior to signing of the informed consent or change in current dose anytime in the 8 weeks prior to signing of the informed consent through Day 1: azathioprine; any antimalarial; mycophenolate mofetil/mycophenolic acid; and oral, SC, or intramuscular methotrexate
- Receipt of any of the following:Azathioprine &gt; 200 mg/day
- Mycophenolate mofetil/mycophenolic acid &gt; 2.0 g/day
- Oral, SC, or intramuscular methotrexate &gt; 25 mg/week
- Any change in the route of administration of oral, SC, or intramuscular methotrexate anytime within the 8 weeks prior to signing of the informed consent
- Receipt of any of the following slow-acting immunosuppressants:Etanercept \le 4 weeks prior to signing the informed consent form
- Adalimumab, infliximab, or golimumab \le 12 weeks prior to signing the informed consent form
- Rituximab or belimumab \le 48 weeks prior to signing the informed consent form
