L403: ## Study 05
L404: Study D3461C00005 (05) was a 52-week, multi-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group study. Patients were randomized in a 1:1:1 fashion to anifrolumab 300mg, anifrolumab 150mg, or placebo (Figure 9). Anifrolumab or placebo was administered as
L405: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L406: an IV infusion via an infusion pump over a minimum of 30 minutes, every 4 weeks for a total of 13 doses. Randomization was stratified using the following factors: SLEDAI-2K score at screening (&lt;10 points vs ≥10 points); week 0 (Day 1) OCS dose (&lt;10 mg/day vs ≥10 mg/day prednisone or equivalent); and results of a type I IFN gene signature test (high vs low).
L407: From week 0 (Day 1) to week 12, patients were permitted only 1 burst of corticosteroids for an increase in SLE disease activity or to control non-SLE related. Patients receiving more than 1 burst during the first 12 weeks of treatment were considered non-responders for subsequent assessments of disease activity, regardless of the reason for the burst (SLE or non-SLE activity).
L408: Permitted medications for SLE standard of care are described in the protocol. Patients who discontinued investigational product (IP) were encouraged to continue to undergo all study-related visits/procedures for the full treatment period (52 weeks) in order to support the final efficacy and safety analysis.
L409: ## **Key Inclusion Criteria:**
L410: - Between 18 and 70 years of age, inclusive, at the time of screening
- Fulfilled at least 4 of the 11 ACR modified 1982 classification criteria for SLE
- Had a diagnosis of pediatric or adult SLE according to the ACR 1982 revised criteria ≥2 4 weeks prior to signing the ICF
- At Screening, disease activity adjudication group confirmation of:SLEDAI-2K Criteria: SLEDAI-2K score ≥6 points and “Clinical” SLEDAI-2K score ≥4 points. The “Clinical” SLEDAI-2K is the SLEDAI-2K assessment score without the inclusion of points attributable to any urine or laboratory results including immunologic measures:Includes points from the following clinical components: arthritis, myositis, rash, alopecia, mucosal ulcers, pleurisy, pericarditis, or vasculitis
- Excludes points attributed to a fever, an SLE headache, and organic brain syndrome
- BILAG-2004 Level Criteria: At least 1 of the following:BILAG-2004 level A disease in ≥1 organ system
- BILAG-2004 level B disease in ≥2 organ systems
- Physician Global Assessment (PGA) score ≥1.0 on a 0 to 3 VAS at screening
- OCS dose stable for at least 2 weeks prior to randomization
- Stable SLE standard of care treatment at the time of randomization
L411: ## **Key Exclusion Criteria:**
L412: - Receipt of any of the following:Azathioprine &gt;200 mg/day
- Mycophenolate mofetil &gt;2 g/day or mycophenolic acid &gt;1.44 g/day
- Oral, SC, or IM methotrexate &gt;25 mg/week
- Mizoribine &gt;150 mg/day

- History of, or current diagnosis of, a clinically significant non SLE-related vasculitis syndrome
- Active severe SLE-driven renal disease where, in the opinion of the PI, protocol specified standard of care was insufficient and utilization of a more aggressive therapeutic approach, such as adding IV cyclophosphamide and/or high dose IV pulse corticosteroid therapy or other treatments not permitted in the protocol, was indicated

L423: **Study 1013:** SRI-4 + OCS tapering at week 24 (evaluated in the whole population and Type I IFN High subgroup), which is a composite of the following:
L424: - Reduction in baseline SLEDAI-2K disease activity score of \ge 4 points
- No worsening in MDGA defined as an increase of \ge 0.3 from baseline on a 0 to 3 visual analog scale
- No worsening in BILAG-2004 defined as \ge 1 new A or \ge 2 new B items compared to baseline
No discontinuation of IP
- Patients who need a burst of OCS or intramuscular corticosteroids need to satisfy the following:No increase in dose of antimalarials or disease modifying anti-rheumatic above Day 1 levels or added new antimalarials/disease modifying anti-rheumatic between Day 1 and Day 169
- No IV corticosteroid between Day 1 and Day 169
- No increase in oral corticosteroids (OCS) above Day 1 dose or intramuscular/intra-articular (IM/IA) injection of corticosteroid after Day 71 and up to Day 169
L425: - No more than one course of burst of corticosteroid between Day 1 and Day 71. One course of burst is defined as burst of OCS above Day 1 dose (up to a maximum dose of 40mg/day prednisone or equivalent and no increase in OCS dose for &gt; 14 days above Day 1 dose) or 1 IM injection of corticosteroids (\le 160 mg methylprednisone or equivalent) or 2 IA injections of corticosteroids
- Reduce OCS to &lt; 10mg/day and \le Day 1 dose of prednisone or equivalent by Day 85 and maintain OCS dose &lt; 10mg/day and \le Day 1 dose until Day 169 (Note: this criterion applies to subjects with OCS \ge 10 mg/day at baseline)
L426: Study 05: SRI-4 at week 52, which is a composite of the following:
L427: - A reduction in baseline SLEDAI-2K disease activity score of \ge 4 points
- No worsening in PGA defined as an increase of \ge 0.3 from baseline on a 0 to 3 PGA visual analog scale
- No worsening in BILAG-2004 defined as \ge 1 new A or \ge 2 new B items compared to baseline
- No discontinuation of IP
- No initiation of restricted medication beyond the protocol-allowed threshold before week 52
L428: Study 04: BICLA at week 52, which is a composite of the following: No worsening from baseline in SLEDAI-2K defined as an increase of &gt; 0 points
L429: - No worsening in PGA defined as an increase of \ge 0.3 from baseline on a 0 to 3 PGA visual analog scale
- Reduction of all baseline BILAG-2004 A to B/C/D and baseline B to C/D and no worsening in other organ systems defined as \ge 1 new A or \ge 2 new B items compared to baseline
- No discontinuation of IP
- No initiation of restricted medication beyond the protocol-allowed threshold before week 52
L430: The original pre-specified endpoint for study 04 was SRI-4 at week 52. See section below on statistical analysis plan for further discussion.
L431: From the construction of these endpoints, the Applicant used a composite strategy in the handling of discontinuation of treatment and/or use of excess immunosuppressants/restricted medication. As supplemental analyses, since we are interested in the efficacy outcomes regardless of treatment discontinuation and/or use of excess immunosuppressants/restricted medication, evaluation of the endpoints using a treatment policy strategy was considered for SRI-4 and BICLA in each of the 3 studies. In other words, to be considered a responder, the patient only needs to satisfy the SLEDAI-2K, BILAG-2004, and MDGA/PGA criteria, irrespective of discontinuation of IP, excess of OCS medication/initiation of restricted medication, or OCS tapering. Demonstrating favorable efficacy of anifrolumab when applying the treatment policy strategy would lessen concerns with differences relating to use of restricted medication rules across the three studies and strengthen the overall confidence in the study results and
L432: conclusions.
L433: From the construction of these endpoints, the Applicant used a composite strategy in the handling of discontinuation of treatment and/or use of excess immunosuppressants/restricted medication. As supplemental analyses, since we are interested in the efficacy outcomes regardless of treatment discontinuation and/or use of excess immunosuppressants/restricted medication, evaluation of the endpoints using a treatment policy strategy was considered for SRI-4 and BICLA in each of the 3 studies. In other words, to be considered a responder, the patient only needs to satisfy the SLEDAI-2K, BILAG-2004, and MDGA/PGA criteria, irrespective of discontinuation of IP, excess of OCS medication/initiation of restricted medication, or OCS tapering. Demonstrating favorable efficacy of anifrolumab when applying the treatment policy strategy would lessen concerns with differences relating to use of restricted medication rules across the three studies and strengthen the overall confidence in the study results and conclusions.

L445: ## Multiplicity control
L446: **Study 1013:** The testing procedures specified that a global trend test (Cochran-Armitage trend test) for the 1000 mg and 300 mg doses would be performed in the overall population and the diagnostic positive subpopulation (i.e., type I IFN test high patients). No multiplicity adjustment for the two populations was applied and alpha was pre-specified to be 0.10. If the test in the overall population was significant at 0.10, then each dose would be compared to placebo at a significance level of 0.10, without specification of order. No other adjustments were planned for the secondary endpoints.
L447: **Study 05:** The primary and key secondary endpoints were tested using a weighted Holm procedure. The procedure applies alpha recycling according to the weights shown in Figure 11 below:

L458: ## Statistical Analysis Plans
L459: ## **Study 1013**
L460: There were two versions of the statistical analysis plan (SAP), with the final version finalized in April 2014. It does not appear that this SAP was reviewed by the Agency.
L461: ## **Primary Endpoint:**
L462: The primary endpoint of SRI-4 + OCS tapering at week 24 was tested in the 1000mg and 300mg arms against placebo in the whole population using the Cochran-Armitage trend test, and likewise both doses were tested in the Type I IFN High subgroup. Subsequently, each dose was nominally tested separately against placebo in each population. Below are described the pre-specified methods used in the analyses.
L463: - Analysis populations:Whole population - Modified Intent-to-Treat (mITT): All randomized subjects who received any investigation product and had a baseline primary efficacy measurement.
- Type I IFN High subgroup: Subset of mITT population who are in the type I IFN High subgroup
L464: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L465: - Handling of week 24 missing data: Missing intermittent week 24 data (complete or partial) were imputed by LOCF. Dropouts with no observed week 20 data were considered as non-responders.
- Analysis methods:Using the mITT: Logistic regression with treatment, screening SLEDAI-2K Score (&lt; 10 points, \ge 10 points), OCS usage at baseline (&lt; 10 mg/day, \ge 10 mg/day), and results of 4-gene diagnostic test at screening (positive, negative) as factors.
- Using the Type I IFN High subgroup: Logistic regression with treatment, screening SLEDAI-2K Score (&lt; 10 points, \ge 10 points), and OCS usage at baseline (&lt; 10 mg/day, \ge 10 mg/day) as factors
L466: For easier comparison and interpretability, the difference of proportions using Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel (CMH) weights adjusted for stratification factors are also reported in the results section.
L467: ## **Exploratory Secondary Endpoints:**
L468: Below are described the pre-specified methods for the analysis of exploratory secondary endpoints.
L469: - All responder endpoints were to be analyzed using a logistic regression with the pre-specified stratification factors. Missing intermittent data were imputed by LOCF, otherwise the patient was considered a non-responder. For easier interpretability of the treatment effects in this review, the difference of proportions using CMH weights are reported instead of the results of the logistic regression. Annual flare rate was analyzed with a negative binomial regression with pre-specified stratification factors and log (follow up time) as an offset variable. Patients lost to follow-up had their times censored at time of dropout.
L470: ## **Study 05 and Study 04**
L471: For study 05, there was 1 version of the SAP, which was finalized in May 2015. This SAP was reviewed by the agency.
L472: For study 04, there were 4 versions of the SAP, with the final version being finalized in July 2019. Two of the versions were reviewed by the agency; the original version and the final version. The final version was updated to reflect the change in the primary and secondary endpoints and restricted medication rules based on the recommendation of the external Steering Committee.
L473: ## **Primary Endpoint:**
L474: Below are described the pre-specified methods for the primary endpoint for study 05 and 04.
L475: - Analysis population: Full analysis set (FAS) defined as patients who were randomized and received at least 1 dose of investigational product.
L476: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L477: - Handling of week 52 missing data (complete or partial): For patients who had an available week 48 observation, this was used to impute week 52, i.e., last observation carried forward (LOCF). If week 48 data is not available, then the patient was considered a non-responder.
- Analysis methods: The difference in proportions was evaluated using Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel weights stratified by screening SLEDAI-2K score (&lt; 10 points, ≥ 10 points), week 0 OCS dose (&lt; 10 mg/day, ≥ 10 mg/day), and results of type I IFN test at screening (high, low).
L478: ## Secondary Endpoints:
L479: Below are described the pre-specified methods for the analysis of secondary endpoints for study 04 and study 05.
L480: - Analysis population: Full analysis set (FAS).
- All responder endpoints were evaluated using a difference in proportions calculated with Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel weights for the pre-specified stratification factors. Missing data were imputed if data from the previous visit was available, otherwise patients were considered to be non-responders.
- Annual flare rate was analyzed with a negative binomial regression with pre-specified stratification factors and log (follow up time) as an offset variable. Patients lost to follow-up had their times censored at time of dropout.

L484: **Study 04:** There was 6 versions of the protocol, with the last version of the protocol being finalized in May 2019. The last version reflected the change in the primary endpoint from SRI-4 to BICLA in with the external Steering Committee after failure of the SRI-4 in study 05. Some major changes to the protocol included changes to the inclusion/exclusion criteria, clarification on restricted medications, unblinding procedures, and study personnel. Further changes included key secondary endpoints and restricted medication rules.
L485: ## Considerations on the Clinical Endpoints
L486: ## Primary Endpoints Considerations:
L487: The question of how best to measure treatment response in SLE remains an open one.25 Current SLE trial endpoints are based on measures of disease activity including the SLEDAI-2K and BILAG. These disease indices are used to calculate a disease activity score, and trial endpoints may be determined based on specifying thresholds for improvement.26 The SLEDAI-2K is a composite global disease activity index developed using consensus techniques in which 24 manifestations are scored across nine organ systems and marked as present or absent over the preceding 30 days according to binary cutoffs.27 Advantages of the SLEDAI-2K include construct validity and the ability to predict major outcomes such as organ damage and mortality.28 However, drawbacks of the SLEDAI-2K include the possibility of different disease patterns to result in the same total score, while improvement or worsening in other organ systems may not be reflected in the score; furthermore, the dichotomous scoring may not allow for variations in the severity of manifestations, which reduces sensitivity to change when used to assess treatment response.29 The BILAG disease activity index is a set of organ-based scales rather than a composite score; the current version (BILAG-2004) comprises of nine organ systems with 97 descriptors, and each is scored as new or recurring, worse, same, improving, or not present during the past 30 days as defined by a glossary. These scores are combined to assign each organ an A–E grade: A (major activity), B (intermediate activity), C (mild or stable disease), D (previous involvement but currently inactive), and E (no previous activity).30 The BILAG index has undergone extensive testing of reliability and validity for the purpose of measuring disease activity; however, it was not developed as a measure of treatment response, and there are limitations to its adaptation for the purpose of a clinical trial endpoint. Although gradations in severity mean that the BILAG-2004 is more sensitive to change than the SLEDAI-2K,31 predefined discrete thresholds may still limit responsiveness to differences between treatment arms in a clinical trial. Another potential drawback of the BILAG-2004 is that it is a
L488: 25 Connelly K, Golder V, Kandane-Rathnayake, and Eric Morand: Clinician-reported outcome measures in lupus trials: a problem worth solving. *Lancet Rheumatology*. Volume 3, Issue 8, E595-E603, August 01, 2021. Published Online June 7, 2021; https://doi.org/10.1016/S2665-9913(21)00119-3.
L489: 26 American College of Rheumatology Ad Hoc Committee Systemic Lupus Erythematosus Response Criteria. The American College of Rheumatology response criteria for systemic lupus erythematosus clinical trials: measures of overall disease activity. *Arthritis Rheum* 2004; 50: 3418–26.
L490: 27 Touma Z, Urowitz MB, Gladman DD. SLEDAI-2K for a 30-day window. *Lupus* 2010; 19: 49–51.
L491: 28 Arora S, Isenberg DA, Castrejon I. Measures of adult systemic lupus erythematosus: disease activity and damage. *Arthritis Care Res (Hoboken)* 2020; 72 (suppl 10): 27–46.
L492: 29 Fortin PR, Abrahamowicz M, Clarke AE, et al. Do lupus disease activity measures detect clinically important change? *J Rheumatol* 2000; 27: 1421–28.
L493: 30 Isenberg DA, Rahman A, Allen E, et al. BILAG 2004. Development and initial validation of an updated version of the British Isles Lupus Assessment Group's disease activity index for patients with systemic lupus erythematosus. *Rheumatology (Oxford)* 2005; 44: 902–06.
L494: 31 Ward MM, Marx AS, Barry NN. Comparison of the validity and sensitivity to change of 5 activity indices in systemic lupus erythematosus. *J Rheumatol* 2000; 27: 664–70.
L495: transitional index requiring comparison of the current state with the previous month to assign improvement or worsening, so two patients with identical current activity can score differently.
L496: The potential limitations of the individual instruments SLEDAI-2K and BILAG-2004 to serve as clinical trial endpoints has led to the development of composite indices such as the SRI and the BICLA, which are currently the most common primary efficacy endpoints used in SLE clinical trials. An SRI response was originally defined as a reduction in SLEDAI of 4 points or more, with the requirement of no BILAG worsening (1A or 2Bs) or deterioration from baseline Physician Global Assessment of 0.3 or more. The SRI was developed on the basis of post-hoc analysis of data from a phase 2 trial of belimumab and was then used as a successful endpoint in two phase 3 belimumab trials.32 Because the SRI responder classification is mostly driven by SLEDAI-2K improvement, i.e. a 4 point improvement in SLEDAI-2K score to achieve the SRI-4 endpoint, it has the potential disadvantage of not being able to detect partial improvements; furthermore, highly weighted disease manifestations can drive the SLEDAI-2K reduction needed for an SRI response while lower weighted domains might not result in the threshold needed for achieving a response. A BICLA response is defined as (1) at least one gradation of improvement in all BILAG A and B scores present at baseline; (2) no new BILAG A, or 2 or more new BILAG B scores; (3) no worsening of total SLEDAI-2K score from baseline; and (4) no deterioration in Physician Global Assessment (\ge 10 mm on a 100 mm scale).33 In contrast to the SRI responder index which requires complete improvement in at least one organ system but not necessarily improvement in all affected organ systems, the BICLA threshold can be achieved not only by complete resolution but also by substantial partial improvement in multiple affected organs. Both SRI-4 and BICLA are considered valid indices,34 however they may demonstrate discordant responses with some subjects achieving one but not the other threshold for improvement. This type of discordance was observed for first phase 3 study of anifrolumab (study 05), in which a number of subjects in the placebo arm achieved improvement on SRI-4 but not BICLA (discussed in more detail below). There is also some evidence that the placebo response rates may be higher with SRI-4 than BICLA.35
L497: The Applicant provided adequate justification for changing the primary endpoint to BICLA in its second phase 3 trial from SRI-4, as described above. The Applicant proposed that this endpoint is more comprehensive, gives more balanced weight to affected body systems, and measure
L498: 32 Furie R, Petri M, Zamani O, et al. A phase III, randomized, placebo-controlled study of belimumab, a monoclonal antibody that inhibits B lymphocyte stimulator, in patients with systemic lupus erythematosus. *Arthritis Rheum* 2011; 63: 3918–30.
L499: 33 Wallace DJ, Strand V, Furie R, et al. Evaluation of treatment success in systemic lupus erythematosus clinical trials: development of the British Isles Lupus Assessment Group-based composite lupus assessment endpoint. 2011 ACR/ARHP Annual Scientific Meeting. Chicago, IL, USA; Nov 8, 2011 (abstr 2265).
L500: 34 Guidance for Industry: Systemic Lupus Erythematosus—Developing Medical Products for Treatment (June 2010); https://www.fda.gov/media/71150/download.
L501: 35 Tselios K, Wakani L, Gladman DD, Su J, Urowitz MB. Response to placebo in non-renal, non-neuropsychiatric systemic lupus erythematosus: a systematic review and pooled analysis. *Rheumatology (Oxford)*. 2021 Jan 5;60(1):73-80.
L502: incremental improvements or worsening. The Applicant also noted that this endpoint retains the SLEDAI-2K, using it (alongside PGA) to exclude patients who worsen in ways not captured by BILAG-2004 from being classified as responders. Furthermore, the FDA guidance34 endorses the use of the BILAG questionnaire stating, “the BILAG is the preferred index to study reduction in disease activity in clinical trials.” Given the endorsement of BILAG in the SLE guidance, the choice of the BICLA primary endpoint for the second anifrolumab phase 3 study was deemed reasonable by the Agency.
L503: ## Secondary Endpoints: CLASI
L504: The Applicant proposed to use the Cutaneous Lupus Erythematosus Disease Area and Severity Index (CLASI) as a secondary endpoint measuring response of SLE cutaneous disease activity to anifrolumab as compared to placebo.36 In consultation with the Division and Dermatology and Dentistry, it has been determined that the CLASI is not a fit-for purpose instrument for measurement of reduction of skin disease in SLE subjects (b) (4) and considers its use exploratory based on the following considerations:
L505: - The CLASI Total Activity Score is driven by the erythema component of the scale, with 39 of a possible 70 total points being potentially attainable just from the erythema score (if each anatomic site received the highest rating of 3; the likelihood of this occurring is unclear). While the extent of cutaneous involvement may be reflective of disease activity, it is not clear that the depth of erythema correlates with the level of activity. Further, the appearance of erythema may be influenced by the inherent depth of an individual's pigmentation, differing in presentation in a fair-skinned person compared to a darkly-pigmented person. It is unclear how the difference in clinical presentation of erythema in different skin types might impact scoring of the depth of erythema. Indeed, erythema in dark skin may even be missed by an untrained observer.
- The eruption of acute cutaneous lupus erythematosus (ACLE), e.g. the butterfly rash of SLE, occurs most frequently during an acute SLE flare. Some authors describe that the lesions of ACLE “can be quite ephemeral, lasting only hours, days, or weeks...” Therefore, for some individuals (i.e. those with transitory lesions), resolution of ACLE may not reflect a positive treatment response, but may, instead, simply be coincident to treatment.
- The CLASI Activity scale does not address lesion morphology (other than mucosal ulcers). For cutaneous lesions other than alopecia, the scale measures three
L506: 36 Albrecht J, Taylor L, Berlin JA, Dulay S, Ang G, et al. The CLASI (Cutaneous Lupus Erythematosus Disease Area and Severity Index): an outcome instrument for cutaneous lupus erythematosus. J Invest Dermatol 125:889–894, 2005.
L507: secondary characteristics: erythema on a three-point scale, scaling on a two-point scale, and verrucosity/hypertrophy as a single point at the extreme for scaling.
L508: 4. The presentations of cutaneous LE are highly variable, and each CLASI score represents a bundling of different cutaneous signs into a single score. Each subject's activity score may represent a different combination of clinical signs. For example, there are several possible ways for arriving at a Total Activity Score of, say, "12": This total could reflect the presence of erythema at several sites; it could reflect erythema with mucous membrane involvement, erythema with scale and alopecia, etc. Therefore, a decrease in CLASI might reflect improvement in different elements of the scale (e.g. improvement in alopecia or erythema) depending on what elements on the scale contributed to an individual's baseline score. Simply reporting a decrease in the CLASI does not permit or present a clinical picture of the nature of improvement, as one would not be able to determine what elements on the CLASI were impacted by study treatment and made for the reduction in score.
L509: Therefore, the review team determined that the CLASI results are not easily interpretable (b) (4)
L510: (b) (4)
L511: ## Secondary Endpoints: FACIT-Fatigue
L512: The Applicant proposed to use the FACIT-Fatigue (FACIT-F) as a secondary endpoint measuring response of SLE patient fatigue to anifrolumab as compared to placebo.37 In consultation with Division of Clinical Outcome Assessment, it has been determined that, while fatigue appears to be an important and common symptom in patients with SLE, the Applicant did not submit sufficient evidence to support interpretation of FACIT-F scores. It is unclear whether changes in the FACIT-F total score in the anifrolumab clinical program are clinically meaningful. The threshold for clinically meaningful within-patient change to assess fatigue severity in the clinical trials could not be estimated using anchor-based methods as the clinical trials did not include appropriate anchor scales. Based on the empirical distribution function (eCDF) curves and item-level analyses, there does not appear to be a difference in the change from baseline at Week 52 in FACIT-F total scores between treatment arms in the phase 3 studies (see Figure 13). FACIT-F items have limitations related to content validity that present additional challenges to interpreting FACIT-F data in the Applicant's clinical trials.
L513: 37 Lai JS, Beaumont JL, Ogale S, Brunetta P, Cella D. Validation of the functional assessment of chronic illness therapy-fatigue scale in patients with moderately to severely active systemic lupus erythematosus, participating in a clinical trial. J Rheumatol. 2011;38(4):672-679.

L540: Similar percentages of patients completed investigational product across the 3 studies: study 1013 (80.1%), study 04 (78.2%), and study 05 (80.1%) (Table 7 and Table 8). In study 04 and study 1013, fewer patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group prematurely discontinued investigational product compared with the placebo group (15.0% vs 28.6% in study 04 and 16.0% vs 25.2% in study 1013). In study 05, similar percentage of patients across the treatment
L541: groups discontinued investigational product prematurely (19.4% in anifrolumab 150 mg, 19.4% in anifrolumab 300 mg, and 20.7% in placebo). Discontinuations due to lack of efficacy were fewer in the anifrolumab 300 mg group compared with placebo in studies 04 and 05 (not recorded in study 1013). In studies 04 and 1013, there were fewer withdrawals by subject/withdrawal of consent in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than in the placebo group.
L542: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L543: A small number of patients had an important protocol deviation (as defined in ICH E3 1995): 14/362 (3.9%) and 18/457 (3.9%) patients in the full analysis sets of studies 04 and 05, respectively, and 10/307 randomized patients (3.3%) in study 1013. Overall, the most frequent important protocol deviations were related to the timing of the laboratory procedures for the PK, investigational product, inclusion criteria, principal investigator oversight/delegation/training, and 'other' reasons. The treatment groups were balanced with respect to both incidence and type of important protocol deviations. With the exception of the 11 patients from the 2 sites that were closed because of sustained non-compliance with protocol procedures, no other patients were excluded from the full analysis sets because of deviations from the protocol. There were no concerns regarding protocol deviations in terms of study conduct or the safety of patients. Also, the numbers and types of protocol deviations were judged not to influence the study outcome.
L544: Specific details for protocol deviations are shown in Table 9, Table 10, and Table 11.

L558: ## Table of Demographic Characteristics
L559: The demographic characteristics of the patients in the 300 mg and placebo arms were broadly similar across the 3 studies submitted to support approval and are representative of the target population of patients with moderate to severe SLE despite standard therapy (Table 12). The mean age was between 39.1 and 43.1 years and the majority of patients were female (91.2% to 93.9%). Approximately 40% of the patients had a BMI of \ge 28 \text{ kg/m}^2. Overall, races were well represented.
L560: All 3 studies included patients from all geographic regions (Table 12). One of the few differences between the studies was the geographic placement of the studies:
L561: - Study 04 had a higher proportion of patients from the Asia-Pacific region (14.6%) compared with studies 05 (5.3%) and 1013 (5.9%) and was the only study to include patients from Japan.
- Study 05 had a higher proportion of patients from Europe (37.9%) compared with
L562: studies 04 (26.8%) and 1013 (25.6%).
L563: - Study 1013 had a higher proportion of patients from Latin America (36.7%) than studies 04 (18.5%) and 05 (13.6%).
- All 3 studies had similar proportions (approximately one-third) of patients from USA/Canada.

L576: ## Other Baseline Characteristics (e.g., disease characteristics, important concomitant drugs)
L577: ## Baseline SLE disease characteristics
L578: The inclusion or exclusion criteria were consistent across the studies, and the baseline SLE disease characteristics of the patients were broadly similar and consistent with a moderate to severe active SLE population (Table 13). At baseline, patients in study 05 had slightly higher mean PGA scores than patients in study 04, indicative of more active disease, and had more moderate to severe skin involvement (CLASI activity score ≥ 10 and BILAG ‘A’ mucocutaneous items). In the two phase 3 studies, baseline SLEDAI-2K organ system involvement were also generally balanced, with the exception of baseline active hematology and immunology higher in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than placebo in study 04 (Table 14). Other than that, the baseline disease characteristics were generally balanced between the studies and between study arms.
L579: In all 3 studies, patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo arms had moderate to severe disease activity at baseline, as indicated by mean SLEDAI-2K scores of 10.7 to 11.5, and 59.6% to 73.4% of patients with a SLEDAI-2K score of ≥ 10; mean BILAG-2004 scores of 18.6 to 19.8; and median PGA scores of 1.68 to 1.87 (Table 13). Overall, 45.0% to 52.5% of patients had severe disease in at least 1 organ system (BILAG-2004 level A), and 41.4% to 50.6% of patients had moderate disease in at least 2 organ systems (BILAG-2004 level B). Patients had a median duration of disease/time since diagnosis of 65.75 to 94.5 months (Table 13).

L591: Referring to organ system components of the SLEDAI-2K, the patients in the 300 mg and placebo arms in studies 04, 05 and 1013 had multi-organ involvement at baseline with the most common organ manifestations of SLE disease being musculoskeletal, mucocutaneous and immunological (Table 14). Based on BILAG-2004 organ items, the most common organ systems with baseline BILAG A or B severity in all three studies (1013, 04 &amp; 05) in the 300 mg and placebo arms were mucocutaneous and musculoskeletal (as shown in Table 13 of the Summary of Clinical Efficacy). Almost all patients had active skin disease (CLASI activity score &gt; 0) with a mean CLASI score of 6.7 to 8.5, and 22.0% to 32.2% of patients in the 300 mg and placebo arms had moderate to severe skin disease (CLASI activity score ≥ 10 points) (Table 13). Across the studies, the majority of patients had active joint manifestations, with 88.9% to 92.4% of patients having swollen joints (median joint counts of 5.0 to 7.0), and 93.3% to 100% of patients having tender joints (median joint counts of 7.0 to 11.0).
L592: The baseline immunology/serology characteristics were similar across the studies. The majority of patients were classified as type I IFN gene signature test high at screening (74.5% to 83.3%) (Table 13). While all patients had to be auto-antibody positive, the baseline results were:
L593: - ANA positivity in most patients (88.9% to 99.0%)
- Anti-dsDNA positivity in 40.1% to 47.8% of patients in studies 04 and 05, and 72.2% to 80.5% of patients in study 1013 (which used the Farr assay, the most specific method for detecting dsDNA antibodies). Note, that study 1013 also used a multiplex assay, but a different type (with different cut-off values) to the multiplex assay used in studies 04 and 05.
- Abnormal (low) C3 levels (28.3% to 42.2%)
- Abnormal (low) C4 levels (19.4% to 27.2%)
L594: ## Baseline SLE-related SOC treatments
L595: All patients were required to be on stable doses of standard of care (SOC) treatments for SLE for an appropriate period of time prior to entering the study. The baseline disease-related SOC treatments across the 3 studies were generally similar and balanced between treatment groups (Table 15 and Table 16).
L596: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation

L625: ## Study 1013
L626: The pre-specified analysis of the primary endpoint of SRI-4 + OCS tapering at week 24 was conducted in the whole population and in the subset of type I IFN test high patients. As alpha was prespecified at 0.10, the odds ratio and corresponding 90% confidence intervals are reported comparing anifrolumab 1000 mg and 300 mg to placebo. To aid in interpretability with study 05 and 04, the 95% confidence intervals are shown. The results were significant in the overall population and in the subset of type I IFN test high patients based on the pre-specified alpha of 0.10, as the p-values from the Cochran-Armitage global trend test were 0.07 and 0.03 respectively. However, no adjustments were made for the evaluation of the endpoint in two populations. The pairwise comparisons of each dose to placebo for the overall population and the subset of type I IFN test high patients are reported in Table 20.

L690: ## Sensitivity Analyses
L691: A tipping point analysis was performed to assess the impact of missing data. This analysis considered the number of patients on placebo and anifrolumab with missing data and varies the possible number of responses among these missing outcomes on the two treatment arms. The goal is to explore the plausibility of scenarios under which the conclusions change, i.e., under which there is no longer evidence of a treatment effect. For a fixed number of additional responders on anifrolumab, we consider how many additional responders on placebo it will take to observe a p-value less than 0.05 to greater 0.05. For example, if the assumption is that there is only 1 additional responder among the missing outcomes on anifrolumab 300mg, the analysis can be used to explore how many additional responders on placebo it would take to tip the results. The plausibility of the scenarios under which conclusions may be tipped can then be assessed.
L692: Further, we make use of the knowledge of whether patients exceeded/initialized restricted medication before the endpoint. By definition of the primary endpoint, such patients were considered non-responders, regardless of how favorable the efficacy outcomes were. Likewise, patients with missing data, but who were known to have exceeded/initialized restricted medication are considered non-responders in the tipping point analysis. By taking this approach, this greatly reduced the number of patients with uncertain response status. However, it should be noted that targeting the treatment policy estimand, which would ignore prior use of excess/initialized restricted medication, would lead to a greater number of patients with unknown response status, and thus the results will be less robust.
L693: Tipping point analyses were performed for all 3 studies on the analysis of SRI-4 and BICLA. As anifrolumab 150 mg and anifrolumab 1000 mg are not being considered for approval, the tipping point analyses only considered comparisons between the 300 mg arm and placebo. Further, to explore all potential effects of missing data, in these analyses, patients who were considered as having available week 52 data based on week 48 LOCF data, are considered as missing.
L694: ## Study 1013
L695: *SRI-4 + OCS tapering at week 24*
L696: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L697: There were 9 patients on the anifrolumab 300mg arm and 20 on the placebo arm that had missing data at week 24. Of these, 19 of them failed to meet the no excess medication and OCS tapering criteria and are considered non-responders. Therefore, based on the definition of the primary endpoint which incorporates use of excess medication and OCS tapering, only 10 patients, 1 on anifrolumab 300 mg and 9 on the placebo arm, are considered as having missing data.
L698: Table 30 below displays the number of additional placebo responders needed to tip results per additional number of added responders on anifrolumab. For example, if we consider the single patient on anifrolumab 300mg with missing data to remain a non-responder, the results do not tip, i.e., does not change the conclusion, until at least 6 of the 9 placebo patients with missing data are responders. If we consider the single patient in the anifrolumab 300mg arm to be a responder, the results do not tip until at least 7 of the 9 placebo patients are responders.
L699: To consider if such scenarios are plausible, we note that among placebo completers at Week 24, the response rate is 20.7%, and the estimated response rate among anifrolumab completers is 37.8%. All scenarios that result in tipping the conclusions would require at least 67% of the placebo subjects with missing data to be responders. Such a response rate would represent almost a doubling of the estimated response rate of anifrolumab 300mg and over three times the response rate observed among placebo completers. Such an increase in response rates for placebo patients who do not have week 24 data seems implausible, and we therefore consider these results to be robust to missing data assumptions.
L700: **Table 30. Study 1013: Tipping point scenarios for SRI-4 + OCS tapering response at week 24**
L701: # of added anifrolumab responders
0	1
# of added placebo responders	5	S	S
6	NS	S
7	NS	NS
8	NS	NS
9	NS	NS

L704: ## **BICLA without OCS tapering at Week 52**
L705: There were 12 patients on the anifrolumab 300mg arm and 33 patients on the placebo arm that had missing week 52 data. Of these, 13 failed to meet the no excess medication requirements
L706: and are considered non-responders. Therefore, the tipping point analysis only considers the 32 patients, 6 on anifrolumab 300 mg, and 26 on placebo, as having missing data.
L707: Table 31 below displays the number of additional placebo responders needed to tip results per additional number of added responders on anifrolumab. For example, given 0 additional responders on anifrolumab 300mg, it would require at least 14 additional responders on placebo to tip the results. Likewise, given 1 additional responder on anifrolumab 300mg, it would require at least 15 additional responders on placebo.
L708: In this study, the response rate at Week 52 is 39.1% among placebo completers, and the estimated response rate among anifrolumab completers is 60.9%. All scenarios that result in tipping the conclusions would require at least 54% of the placebo subjects with missing data to be responders. If we further consider that at least some additional anifrolumab 300mg responders could be expected, an even higher response rate would be required among missing placebo subjects to tip the results. Considering the higher proportion of missing placebo subjects required to be responders to tip the results relative to the observed rates among placebo completers, we consider this to be a relatively unlikely scenario.

L714: ## BICLA

Therefore, the tipping point analyses considers the 51 patients, 26 on anifrolumab 300mg, and 25 on placebo, as having missing data.
L717: Table 32 below displays the number of additional placebo responders needed to tip results per additional number of added responders on anifrolumab. For example, given 0 additional responders on anifrolumab 300mg, it would require at least 13 additional responders on placebo.
L718: Based on placebo completers, an estimated response rate is 36.0%, and the estimated response rate among anifrolumab completers in this study is 56.8%. All scenarios that result in tipping the conclusions would require at least 52% of the placebo subjects with missing data to be responders. If we further consider that at least some of the 26 missing anifrolumab 300mg subjects could be responders, based on these observed rates, an even higher response rate would be required among missing placebo subjects to tip the results. Considering the higher proportion of missing placebo subjects required to be responders to tip the results relative to the observed rates among placebo completers, we consider this to be a relatively unlikely scenario.

L794: ## Subgroups
L795: This section summarizes results from various subgroup analyses for the primary endpoint, BICLA response at week 52 within subgroup levels. The subgroups and levels explored are:
L796: - Sex (Male; Female)
- Age (&lt;45; \ge45)
- Race (White; Non-White)
- Region (North America; Not North America)
L797: BICLA response at week 52 is considered for each study. We further performed shrinkage analyses for sex, age, race, and region. Bayesian hierarchical modeling produces shrinkage estimates of the individual study treatment effects by removing the within study variability. Further, treatment effects are regarded as exchangeable, which allows them to be different but related. Therefore, shrinkage estimates tend to be more precise and provide narrower confidence/credible intervals. Below is the model used:
L798: (p_A - p_p)_i \sim N((p_A - p_p)_i, \sigma_i^2), i = 1, 2(p_A - p_p)_i \sim N(p_A - p_p, \tau^{-2}), i = 1, 2p_A - p_p \sim N(0, 2), \quad \tau^{-2} \sim \text{Gamma} N(0.001, 0.001)
L799: We assume that before seeing data, the difference in proportions is 0, and we further place one-eighth of a response (BICLA) and one-eighth of a non-response on anifrolumab 300mg and placebo, which will not be very impactful in linking the groups together. Thus,
L800: \text{Var}(p_A - p_p) = \frac{\tilde{p}(1 - \tilde{p})}{n_A} + \frac{\tilde{p}(1 - \tilde{p})}{n_p} = 2
L801: Where \tilde{p} is the common proportion
L802: ## Study 1013
L803: Figure 14 display the forest plots of the subgroup analyses (in isolation and shrinkage) for sex, age, race, and region. Consistent treatment effects were observed across each of the subgroups. Among males and patients over 45, the nominal 95% confidence intervals include 0 for both the sample and shrinkage analyses. This is not surprising since the study was not powered for any of the subgroup analyses and relatively few patients were males.
L804: **Figure 14. Forest plot of subgroup analyses for study 1013**
L805: Source: Statistical Reviewer's Analysis
Treatment effects are measured by difference in proportions
Values greater than 0 favor anifrolumab

L814: ## Dose/Dose Response
L815: Figure 17 below shows the change in SLEDAI-2K, a measure of disease activity that is used to define both SRI-4 and BICLA, over time from baseline for study 05. The placebo group had the smallest reduction, followed by anifrolumab 150mg, while anifrolumab 300mg had the largest reduction throughout all timepoints beginning at week 8 throughout the remainder of the study. While study 05 failed on the primary endpoint of SRI-4, both under the original and revised set of study 04 restricted medication rules, and while the differences in SLEDAI-2K relative to placebo are not statistically significant, these results support a dose-dependent trend.

L823: ## 8.1.3. Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
L824: Evaluation of efficacy was evaluated as stand-alone trials. Pooled efficacy analyses were not considered during this review.
L825: ## Additional Efficacy Considerations
L826: The Applicant was asked to provide an explanation for the apparent discrepancy between SRI-4 and BICLA responses within study 05 and between study 04 and study 05. These two studies were identical in design and enrolled patients had similar demographics, baseline SLE disease characteristics and background medication use (see above Table 12, Table 13, Table 14, and Table 16). However, while study 04 showed differences of anifrolumab over placebo for both BICLA and SRI-4 endpoints (Table 27 and Table 28), study 05 only showed differences using the BICLA endpoint (Table 25 and Table 26).
L827: In order to further understand the discrepant result on SRI(4) in study 05, the Applicant performed an exploratory post-hoc analysis to compare each patient's outcome on SRI(4) and BICLA for each patient in the studies, with the following findings.
L828: - The majority of patients in both studies (84% to 85%) had concordant outcomes on SRI(4) and BICLA (i.e., had a SRI[4] response and BICLA response, or a SRI[4] nonresponse and BICLA nonresponse), and the pattern of concordance was consistent across the studies.In study 05, the difference in the proportion of patients who were both SRI[4] responders and BICLA responders ('dual responders') between anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo was 14.5% (95% CI (4.6, 24.0)), which is clinically meaningful and similar to that observed in study 04 [16.9%; 95% CI (7.2, 26.7)].
- A smaller proportion of patients in both studies had discordant outcomes on SRI(4) and BICLA (i.e., had a SRI[4] response and BICLA nonresponse, or a SRI[4] nonresponse and BICLA response). In one subset in study 05 (SRI[4] responders/BICLA nonresponders; 11.0% of the total patients in the study), more patients in the placebo group were able to achieve a SRI(4) response than patients in the anifrolumab group (15.2% vs 6.7%), and this appears to be driving the overall discrepant result on SRI(4) in the study.In this discordant subset in study 05, patients in the placebo group had lower baseline disease severity, and greater steroid use during the study, compared with the anifrolumab 300 mg group. This may have led to more patients in the placebo group achieving complete resolution of arthritis, which alone meets the criteria for a SRI(4) response, leading to more SRI(4) responders in the placebo group compared with the anifrolumab 300 mg group.
L829: - Despite achieving a SRI(4) response, all the patients in this discordant subset in study 05 were BICLA nonresponders. To be a BICLA responder, a patient must have improvement in all impacted organ systems at baseline and, as the patients in this subset had several involved clinical manifestations at baseline and none of them achieved improvement in all of them, they were all BICLA nonresponders.
L830: We note that these results should be interpreted with caution as they are from exploratory analyses and in some cases based on a subgroup of patients defined by post-randomization variables. There was a clinically meaningful difference between the treatment groups in the proportion of patients who were both a SRI(4) responder and BICLA responder, in favor of anifrolumab, in both studies. These dual responders, based on 2 validated endpoints, confirm the beneficial effect of anifrolumab on overall disease activity in patients with SLE. One subset of patients in study 05 appears to be driving the overall discrepant result on SRI(4) in study 05. In this subset, more patients in the placebo group were able to achieve a SRI(4) response than patients in the anifrolumab group. This may be due to an imbalance between the treatment groups in baseline disease severity, and the degree of steroid use during the study, leading to more patients in the placebo group achieving complete resolution of arthritis, which alone meets the criteria for a SRI(4) response.
L831: ## 8.1.4. Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L832: Study 04 represents an adequate and well-controlled trial showing favorable and statistically significant changes on the pre-specified, clinically meaningful primary and secondary endpoints when comparing anifrolumab 300 mg IV versus placebo. Because the phase 2 trial (1013) was designed only as a proof of concept and dose-ranging study with inadequate multiplicity control and study 05, the first phase 3 trial, failed on its primary endpoint, these cannot be considered additional successful adequate and well-controlled trials when considered independently. However, the review team feels that when these trials are considered in combination, the consistent results observed in studies 1013 and 05 do provide confirmatory evidence that support the effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV.
L833: Across the three studies, there are three randomized comparisons, each with adequate duration (52 weeks), that support the claim that anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher BICLA response rates compared to placebo. Further, two of the three studies provide support that anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher SRI-4 response rates compared to placebo at week 52. While study 05 failed to show an effect on SRI-4, the study showed a numerically dose-dependent trend that is not statistically significant in SLEDAI-2K, a measure of disease activity that is used to define both SRI-4 and BICLA and for which there was relatively consistent evidence across all three studies. In all three studies, additional supportive evidence was noted in the analyses of the secondary endpoints, including a greater proportion of subjects on anifrolumab than placebo able to reduce oral corticosteroid use and a lower proportion of subjects on anifrolumab experiencing a flare of SLE disease activity, further supporting the effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV.

L858: infections	normally non-pathogenic or rarely pathogenic in individuals with normal immune function or cause an infection of a type or severity not seen in the normal host. PTs included in a MedDRA custom PT grouping, developed with reference to Winthrop et al 2015 and the NIH Guidelines for the Prevention and Treatment of Opportunistic Infections in HIV-Infected Adults and Adolescents.
Herpes Zoster	A viral infection characterized by a cutaneous vesicular eruption on an erythematous base presenting along dermatome(s) and usually associated with prodromal pain.	AES of herpes zoster included serious and non-serious AES identified by select PTs from the HLT Herpes viral infections.
TB (including latent TB)	An active TB infection could be either new or a reactivation of dormant disease and could be either bacteriologically confirmed or clinically diagnosed. Latent TB was defined as a mycobacterial infection without clinical, bacteriological findings, or radiologic findings consistent with active TB and a TB blood test using an IGRA or using purified protein derivative skin test that is positive both at the time of provisional diagnosis and on repeat assessment.	AES of TB or latent TB were identified using a custom MedDRA PT grouping (PTs: latent tuberculosis, tuberculosis, and mycobacterium tuberculosis complex test positive).
Influenza	Where a patient reported a viral infection severe enough to be considered, a viral test should have been performed to confirm the diagnosis. However, if in the opinion of the investigator, the subject had influenza based on defined clinical symptoms, this was to be reported as influenza, whether or not a test to confirm the diagnosis had been performed. Custom MedDRA PT grouping (PTs: anaphylactic reaction, anaphylactic shock, anaphylactoid reaction, and anaphylactoid shock).

Custom MedDRA PT grouping (LLT hypersensitivity reaction and PT hypersensitivity).
Infusion-related reaction	Infusion-related reaction was defined as any other reaction (aside from anaphylaxis or hypersensitivity) occurring during infusion of IP or felt to be temporally related to the infusion within 24 hours of IP administration.	AES with the PT infusion-related reaction.
Malignancy	A neoplasm characterized by cells with abnormal features and uncontrolled rapid growth with invasive and/or metastatic tendencies, diagnosed based on pathologic and clinical standards. Investigators were requested to additionally obtain biopsy results and pertinent biomarker and/or genetic testing results.	Four SMQs: hematological malignant tumors, non-hematological malignant tumors, malignant lymphomas (narrow), and skin malignant tumors (narrow).
MACE	An independent CV-EAC reviewed cases of interest to determine if they met accepted diagnostic criteria for MACE events.	For the phase III studies, MACE were those assessed by the CV-EAC. For study 1013, MACE were identified using a custom MedDRA term list.
L859: Vasculitis (non-SLE)	An inflammatory disorder of blood vessels involving arteries and/or veins and characterized by characteristic clinical signs/symptoms and diagnosed by biopsy, imaging such as angiography, or blood tests such as findings of antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies consistent with the diagnosis. Underlying causes were to be identified, wherever possible. Only non-SLE-related vasculitis was to be reported as an AESI of vasculitis.

L861: ## 8.2.2. Review of the Safety Database
L862: ## Overall Exposure
L863: In the entire clinical development program for anifrolumab, which includes scleroderma and lupus nephritis in addition to SLE, more than 1,000 subjects have been exposed to at least one dose of anifrolumab (doses ranging from 150 mg to 1000 mg IV). For SLE specifically, 837 SLE patients (Table 44) have received at least one dose of anifrolumab (doses ranging from 150 mg to 1000 mg). The proposed dose for SLE is 300 mg IV every 4 weeks. For the proposed dose of 300 mg in SLE patients, a total of 639 subjects have received at least one dose (Table 45), of which 180 were initially treated with placebo during the 52-week placebo-controlled periods of studies 1013, 04, and 05 and then switched to anifrolumab 300 mg in study 1145 (open-label extension study from 1013) or study 09 (long-term extension study from studies 04 and 05).

L884: ## Adequacy of the safety database:
L885: Regarding overall exposure for the assessment of safety, a total of 1029 subjects have been exposed to *any dose* of anifrolumab for periods of up to 4 years in the anifrolumab clinical development program. Of those 1029 subjects, at least 837 were SLE patients exposed to IV anifrolumab Q4W (150 mg, 300 mg, or 1000 mg). Of these, 688 patients with SLE were exposed to IV anifrolumab for at least one year and 263 patients were exposed for at least 3 years. 459 subjects were exposed to at least one dose of anifrolumab 300 mg during the 52-week, placebo-controlled periods of studies 1013, 04 and 05; of those, 420 (91.5%) of subjects were exposed to 300 mg IV anifrolumab for at least 6 months, and 315 (68.6%) were exposed to 300 mg IV anifrolumab for at least one year. Data from the ongoing long-term extension study (09) for &gt; 150 subjects exposed to anifrolumab 300 mg for at least 3 years provides additional long-term safety information to support the BLA. Patients who received placebo in the phase 3 studies and enrolled in study 09 were randomized to either continue placebo or receive 300 mg of anifrolumab. The size of the safety database for this submission is adequate to provide sufficient basis for a risk/benefit assessment in the adult SLE population.
L886: ## 8.2.3. Adequacy of Applicant's Clinical Safety Assessments
L887: ## Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
L888: The data quality submitted was well-organized and adequate to perform a complete review of safety. Several information requests were sent to the Applicant during the review of safety for additional analyses which were satisfactory and received in a timely manner.
L889: ## Categorization of Adverse Events
L890: Verbatim terms of AEs and Disease Related Events (DRE) recorded in the case report forms (CRF) by investigators were coded by the Applicant using MedDRA dictionary Preferred Term (PT), High-Level Term (HLT), and System Organ Class (SOC) version 22.1. The MedDRA coding of the information generated from studies 1013, 1145, 04, 05, and 09 conducted by the Applicant was generally acceptable. Additionally, the clinical lab and vital sign ranges for clinically significant abnormal results was reviewed and appeared to be appropriate.
L891: ## Routine Clinical Tests
L892: The following clinical and lab testing were included in studies 1013, 04, 05 and 09 in support of anifrolumab IV's safety profile:
L893: - Symptom driven physical exam, height, and weight
- Vital signs: systolic and diastolic blood pressure, heart rate and temperature
- Complete cell count (CBC) with differential and platelet count, hemoglobin, and hematocrit
- Serum chemistries: albumin, alkaline phosphatase, ALT, AST, BUN, calcium, chloride, creatinine, creatine kinase, glucose, gamma glutamyl transferase, potassium, sodium, total bilirubin, cholesterol, and fasting lipid profile (HDL, LDL, triglycerides)
- Urinalysis: including specific gravity, spot urine protein, spot urine creatinine, and protein/creatinine ratio
- Pregnancy test
- TB blood test
- Immunology profile: Anti-dsDNA, C3, C4, CH50
- Immunogenicity test (pre-dose)
- 12-lead ECG: baseline and end of study
- Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS)
L894: ## 8.2.4. Safety Results
L895: ## Deaths
L896: Overall, there were 14 deaths during the anifrolumab clinical studies in the anifrolumab development program for SLE including all doses for 52 weeks (studies 1013, 05 and 04) and placebo controlled long-term extension study 09 (participants in studies 05 and 04 continuing
L897: on either anifrolumab or placebo for greater than 52 weeks). Key patient data for patients with fatal AEs are summarized in Table 48, based on full patient narratives of all deaths in the CSRs for completed studies, in the 120-day safety update for the phase 3 long-term extension study (study 09, LTE), and in an April 2021 response to query regarding the DSUR covering January 2020-2021. No deaths occurred during the phase 2 studies 02 and 08 or during phase 1 studies (MI-CP180 and 06). Four deaths occurred during the phase 2 and 3 52-week placebo controlled studies (1013, 04 and 05)—one in the anifrolumab 1000 mg arm of study 1013, one in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm of study 04, one in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm of study 05, and one in the placebo arm of study 05. There was one death during the open-label extension (OLE) of the phase 2 study (1145) in a subject originally randomized to placebo in study 1013 and who received first 1000 mg anifrolumab (16 doses) and then 300 mg anifrolumab (16 doses) during the OLE period. For the phase 3 LTE through August 1, 2019 with the initial BLA filing, there were two deaths, both in the placebo group. Between August 2019 and March 19, 2020, two additional deaths were recorded in the 120-day safety update of the phase 3 LTE; both in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm, including one attributed to infection (pneumonia). Five additional deaths were reported for study 09 in an Annual Report/DSUR for study 09 covering January 2020 through January 2021, all in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm, four attributed to infections (1 pneumonia and 3 COVID-19) and one attributed to SLE exacerbation.

L900: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L901: Source: CSR/narratives for studies 1013, 1145, 04, 05, and 09 (including 120-day safety update);
response-to-query-fda-2021-patient-narratives (4/07/2021)
L902: Overall, 11 patients in the all anifrolumab group and 3 patients in the placebo group had a fatal adverse event. Many of the deaths occurred during the long-term extension study from the two phase 3 studies (study 09), thus exposure time to anifrolumab was significantly greater than placebo, resulting in overall similar exposure-adjusted incidence rate (EAIR) for deaths. The mortality EAIR for anifrolumab was 0.3/100 PY compared to 0.6/100 PY (data not shown). A number of deaths in the all anifrolumab group were associated with a serious infection, including 5 due to pneumonia and 3 COVID-19, as compared to one (encephalitis) in the placebo group, as summarized in Table 49. Limiting the data to only subjects who were either on anifrolumab IV 300 mg vs placebo for 52+ weeks, there were 7 deaths attributable to infections (4 pneumonia and 3 COVID-19).

L907: ## Serious Adverse Events
L908: ## Serious adverse events: 52-week treatment period
L909: In a pool of the three studies submitted to support approval (1013, 04 and 05), fewer patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group had any Serious Adverse Event (SAE) than patients in the placebo group (Table 50; 11.8% vs 16.7%); the EAIR risk difference was -7.2 (95% CI -12.5, -1.9). Similarly, in a pool limited to the two phase 3 studies (04 and 05), fewer patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group had any Serious Adverse Event (SAE) than patients in the placebo group (11.1% vs 16.4%); the EAIR risk difference was -7.3 (95% CI: -13.3, -1.4). Among patients with SAEs during the 52-week treatment period, in both treatment groups, the most common SAEs by PT were pneumonia and systemic lupus erythematosus.
L910: There were 8 patients (1.7%) in the anifrolumab group and 9 patients (1.9%) in the placebo group who had an SAE of pneumonia (EAIR 1.9 vs 2.2; risk difference -0.3, 95% CI -2.5, 1.8). Among the 8 SAEs on pneumonia in the anifrolumab 300 mg group, two were fatal (see details above) and there were no fatal pneumonias in the placebo group.
L911: As a safety variable, the PT of systemic lupus erythematosus included SLE flares, where subjects experienced increased disease activity with new or worsening clinical signs and symptoms. There were 7 patients (1.5%) in the anifrolumab group and 14 patients (3.0%) in the placebo group who had an SAE of systemic lupus erythematosus (EAIR 0.7 vs 3.5; risk difference -1.8, 95% CI (-4.3, 0.4).
L912: One case of SAE-hypersensitivity and two cases of SAE-angioedema occurred during treatment with anifrolumab 300 mg for a total of 0.6% (3/459) of patients experiencing hypersensitivity events during the 52-week clinical trial periods of the three studies submitted for approval. There was one event of anaphylactic reaction reported in the SLE development program in a subject receiving anifrolumab 150 mg (study 05).
L913: In the one reported case of anaphylaxis with anifrolumab - patient (b)(6) in study D3461C00005 - a 27-year old white female from Argentina experienced a mild pruritic rash after the first dose of study treatment with anifrolumab 150 mg infusion. The patient was prophylactically administered one dose of diphenhydramine prior to the second infusion. On the day of Dose 2, during the post-dose flush infusion, the patient developed anaphylactic reaction with dyspnea, wheezing, flushing, and pruritis; was treated with hydrocortisone, diphenhydramine, and epinephrine; and was hospitalized for anaphylactic reaction. Both the initial pruritic rash and anaphylactic reaction were considered to be related to study treatment with anifrolumab.

L919: ## **Serious adverse events: Phase 3 long-term extension treatment period (data not shown)**
L920: The phase 3 LTE group (study 09, including 120-day safety update) was similar to the 52-week treatment period, where SAEs were lower in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo group: 21.7% vs 22.2%; EAIR 10.1 vs 17.2 (risk difference -7.1; 95% CI -11.2, -3.2). Also consistent with the 52-week safety pool, the most common SAEs in both treatment groups in the phase 3 LTE period were pneumonia and systemic lupus erythematosus. Pneumonia was balanced between the two groups (9 (2.5%) in anifrolumab vs 10 (2.7%) in placebo (EAIR 1.0 vs 1.9; difference -0.9, 95% CI -2.6, 0.4)). Systemic lupus erythematosus flare/worsening SAE was less common in the anifrolumab group as compared to the placebo group (6 (1.7%) in anifrolumab vs 15 (4.1%) in placebo (EAIR 0.7 vs 2.9; difference -2.2, 95% CI -4.1, -0.9)). During up to 4 years of treatment, the EAIR of systemic lupus erythematosus SAEs was lower in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo group.
L921: The evaluation of SAEs by yearly intervals did not suggest any change in overall SAE incidence of the course of long-term treatment. Evaluation of the most common SAEs in the anifrolumab 300 mg group by yearly intervals showed similar event rates over time (Table 51).

L926: ## Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
L927: Overall, in the 52-week treatment period in the three studies submitted to support approval as well as in the phase 3 long-term extension data, there was low incidence of discontinuation from investigational product (IP) due to adverse event/s that was similar across treatment groups (Table 52). The proportion of patients with an adverse event leading to discontinuation of IP (DAEs) was low and favored the anifrolumab 300 mg group (4.1%) over the placebo group (5.2%); EAIR 4.5 vs 6.0 (risk difference -1.4 (95% CI -4.7, 1.7)). The most commonly reported DAEs by SOC in the anifrolumab group were Infections and Infestations with no PT predominating; the most commonly reported DAEs by SOC in the placebo group were Musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders all in PT of systemic lupus erythematosus (flare). There was no evidence for an increase in rate of discontinuations over time in the phase 3 long-term safety data. As observed during the 52-week treatment period, rates of DAEs in the phase 3 long-term safety data (including 120-day safety update) were low and similar between the anifrolumab 300 mg group (EAIR 2.6/100 PY) and placebo group (4.2/100 PY) (data not shown).

L932: ## Increased Incidence in anifrolumab 300 mg of DAEs in the SOC group nervous system disorders
L933: The second most commonly reported DAE by SOC in the anifrolumab group were Nervous system disorders with no PT predominating (Table 52)—5 (1.1%) cases in the anifrolumab 300 mg group vs 1 (0.2%) case in the placebo group; EAIR 1.2 vs 0.2 (risk difference 0.9; 95% CI -0.3, 2.5). In the phase 3 long-term extension pool (including 120-day safety update), which included only subjects receiving anifrolumab 300 mg or placebo for both their initial 52-week treatment period as well as long-term extension period up to 4 years, there was a similar imbalance of DAEs with 6 DAEs in the SOC of nervous system disorders in the anifrolumab group and 0 in the placebo group, again with no PT predominating (Table 53).

L936: Two of the 3 studies submitted to support approval provide potential data on dose-relatedness for DAEs. In study 05, the 150 mg dose of anifrolumab was studied in parallel to the 300 mg dose in addition to the placebo arm. One patient in the placebo arm of study 05 was recorded with a DAE for SOC of nervous system disorders (PT neuropsychiatric lupus), while 3 were recorded in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm (no PT predominating) and 0 recorded in the anifrolumab 150 mg arm. In the phase 2 study/1013, 1 DAE for SOC of nervous system disorders was recorded in the placebo arm (optic neuritis) and 1 DAE for SOC of nervous system disorders was recorded in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm (transverse myelitis); 0 DAE for SOC of nervous system disorders recorded in the anifrolumab 1000 mg arm.
L937: For total treatment-emergent adverse events, the SOC of nervous system disorders was common in both placebo and anifrolumab treatment arms (ranging from 14-31%), with headache as the predominating PT. 52-week data from studies 1013, 04 and 05 showed an imbalance with SOC nervous system disorders occurring in 19.4% (EAIR 23.9) of anifrolumab 300 mg subjects and 16.7% (EAIR 21.7) of placebo subjects. The phase 3 LTE data (including 120-day safety update) also showed an imbalance with SOC nervous system disorders occurring in 31.1% of anifrolumab 300 mg subjects (EAIR 16.6) and 18.1% of placebo subjects (EAIR 14.5). In study 05, TEAE for SOC nervous system disorders was higher in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm (23.3%; EAIR 26.1) than the anifrolumab 150 mg arm (14%; EAIR 15.6); placebo arm was lower than anifrolumab 300 mg arm but higher than the anifrolumab 150 mg arm (14%, EAIR 19.7). Study 1013 did not show an imbalance between study arms for SOC nervous system disorders—24% (EAIR 34.3) in the placebo group; 21% in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm; 20% in the anifrolumab 1000 mg arm (EAIR 26.2).
L938: Serious adverse events (SAE) for the SOC of nervous system disorders were uncommon (&lt;2%) for any of the anifrolumab treatment doses during the 52-week treatment period. For the 300 mg dose of anifrolumab during 52-weeks, SAEs were balanced between anifrolumab and placebo groups—4 (0.9%)/EAIR 1.0 vs 5 (1.0%)/EAIR 1.3. A higher number of SAE for SOC nervous system disorders occurred in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo group in the phase 3 long-term safety data—7 (1.9%)/EAIR 0.8 vs 1 (0.3%)/EAIR 0.2. The 7 SAEs included 2 cases of post-herpetic neuralgia and 1 case each of hypoesthesia, intracranial aneurysm, ischemic stroke, myasthenia gravis, and syncope.
L939: The SAE/DAE of myasthenia gravis occurred during study 05 in a 67 year old female 22 days after her fifth dose of anifrolumab 300 mg. The SAE/DAE of transverse myelitis occurred during study 1013 in a 51 year old female 3 days after her second dose of anifrolumab 300 mg; this patient was found to have positive CSF PCR for varicella and the transverse myelitis was ultimately attributed to Herpes Zoster (and resolved after treatment with steroids and acyclovir). Two cases of SAE/DAE post-herpetic neuralgia occurred during the long-term extension study. Thus, 3 SAE/DAE cases for anifrolumab 300 mg in the SOC of nervous system disorders were directly associated with Herpes Zoster infection (1 in 52-week data, 2 in long-term extension data).
L940: ## Significant Adverse Events
L941: Table 54 is a tabular listing of adverse events observed during the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05) by treatment group that were rated as severe by study investigators. A slightly higher proportion of subjects in the anifrolumab 300 mg group experienced severe treatment emergent adverse events as compared to the placebo group—9.2% vs 7.9%; EAIR 10.5 vs 9.5. The most common severe AEs during the 52-week period by SOC were infections and infestations, with no particular PT predominating. Similar overall results were observed in the long-term extension data (Table 55), with a higher proportion of severe AEs in the anifrolumab arm as compared to placebo (15.3% vs 9.3%), but an equivalent exposure-adjusted incidence rate in both arms (EAIR 6.8 for anifrolumab 300mg and EAIR 6.7 for placebo). Infections and infestations remained the most common severe SOC with PTs of pneumonia and herpes zoster being the most common: Anifrolumab 300 mg group had 6 cases of severe pneumonia and 4 cases of severe herpes zoster as compared to 3 cases of severe pneumonia and 0 cases of severe herpes zoster in the placebo group.

L954: ## Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
L955: Most subjects experienced a treatment emergent adverse event (TEAE) during the 52-week period of studies 1013, 04 and 05. Adverse events were reported in 87% of patients receiving anifrolumab 300 mg and 79% of patients receiving placebo (Source: Table 3.2.1.1.1). Table 56
L956: lists the frequency of the TEAEs observed during the 52-week period of studies 1013, 04, and 05 by SOC and treatment group. For the most commonly occurring TEAEs (\ge 5\%), the proportion of subjects who had a TEAE were somewhat higher in the anifrolumab group than the placebo group for PTs of nasopharyngitis, upper respiratory tract infection, bronchitis, infusion related reaction, herpes zoster, back pain, and cough (Table 56). Overall, the types and incidences of common TEAEs were consistent with what would be expected for patients with active SLE who had been exposed to immunosuppressive therapies and are similar to what has been observed in other SLE clinical trials.

L961: ## Laboratory Findings
L962: ## Hematology
L963: Patients with treatment-emergent changes in clinical hematology and chemistry tests (TELVC) that fell outside predefined criteria are considered clinically meaningful. The mean hematology values were generally similar between the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups at baseline and at Week 52 in the phase 3 studies (data not shown). There were no clinically meaningful differences noted between the treatment groups in categorical shifts (data not shown). During the phase 3 52-week study period, there were no notable differences among treatment groups in the incidence of any potentially clinically important hematology abnormalities based on prespecified criteria (Table 57). The most frequently observed potentially clinically important hematology abnormalities were for lymphocytes, with values \le 0.5 GI/L below extended reference range observed in 10.4% of patients in the anifrolumab mg group versus 22.4% in the placebo group. There were also no significant differences in the frequency or pattern of potentially clinically significant treatment-emergent changes from baseline in hematology variables during the phase 3 long-term safety data (Table 57). Similar results were seen in the phase 2 study (1013) 52-week study period (data not shown), including a lower number of subjects in the anifrolumab 300 mg than placebo group with below normal lymphocyte count at week 52—5.5% for anifrolumab 300 mg vs 26.8% for placebo.

L970: ## Clinical Chemistry
L971: In the phase 3 safety pool, no significant differences in mean clinical chemistry values were noted between anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo and no clinically meaningful trends in change from baseline values over time were detected (data not shown). Renal function as assessed by serum creatinine remained stable from baseline to week 52. There were generally no differences noted between the treatment groups in the frequency and pattern of potentially clinically significant changes or categorical shifts from baseline in clinical chemistry variables during the phase 3 studies at 52 weeks or in the phase 3 long-term safety data (Table 58). Two exceptions were: 1) a slight increase in incidence of elevated gamma glutamyl transferase during both the 52-week safety period (2.8 % for anifrolumab 300 mg vs 1.1% for placebo) and phase 3 long-term safety period (3.1% for anifrolumab 300 mg vs 1.4% for placebo: and 2) a slight increase in incidence of elevated glucose during the phase 3 long-term safety study (7.7% for anifrolumab 300 mg vs 5.5 % for placebo in phase 3 LTE vs 4.9% and 4.1%, respectively, during the 52-week study period). 52-week safety data from the phase 2 study (1013) showed no significant changes in chemistry values that were different between anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo (data not shown).

L977: In the two phase 3 studies, the change from baseline to week 52 in mean specific gravity was similar in the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups (data not shown). Urine protein/creatinine ratio (UPCR) values remained stable from baseline to week 52 (data not shown). There was also no difference noted between the treatment groups in the frequency and pattern of potentially clinically significant changes in UPCR over 52 weeks of treatment. In the phase III long-term safety data, the change from baseline to the end of treatment in mean specific gravity was similar in the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups (data not shown). From baseline to end of treatment, the change in median UPCR values was -0.12 and 0.46 mg/mmol in the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups, respectively (data not shown). In the phase III long-term safety data, 2.2% and 2.8% of patients had a UPCR value categorized as a potentially meaningful increase (\ge 395 mg/mmol creatinine) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and placebo group, respectively (data not shown).
L978: Additional safety data available from study 05 comparing two doses of anifrolumab (150 mg vs 300 mg) may indicate if there were any dose-related patterns for clinically meaningful changes in hematology and chemistry parameters. There were no notable differences among treatment groups in the incidence of any potentially clinically important hematology abnormalities based on prespecified criteria. The most frequently observed potentially clinically important hematology abnormalities were for lymphocytes, with values \le 0.5 G/L below extended reference range observed in 20.4% and 11.7% of patients in the anifrolumab 150 mg and 300 mg groups, respectively, versus 20.8% in the placebo group (Table 59). These results are similar to what was observed in the combined phase 3 52-week and long-term safety data (Table 57) and were not unexpected for this patient population; the majority of these changes were assessed to be not clinically important. For other hematology parameters (hemoglobin, hematocrit, leukocytes, monocytes, neutrophils, and platelets), few patients had potentially clinically important abnormalities.
