L992: ## Vital Signs
L993: During the 52-week study period in the two phase 3 studies, no differences in mean blood pressure or heart rate were noted between anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo. There were also no clinically meaningful trends in change from baseline values at 52 weeks (data not shown). There were also patients with clinically significant treatment-emergent changes in diastolic blood pressure, systolic blood pressure, or heart rate (data not shown). Similar results were seen in the phase 2 52-week data (data not shown). In studies 1013, 04 and 05, there were no clinically important differences among treatment groups in mean changes from baseline in body weight to week 52.
L994: ## Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
L995: In all three studies submitted to support approval (1013, 04 and 05), there were no shifts in ECG parameters from normal to abnormal that were clinically significant and the proportions of patients with abnormal (not clinically significant) ECG findings were balanced between the anifrolumab 300 mg group and the placebo group (data not shown). Very few patients in either treatment group had ECG results fulfilling potentially clinically significant criteria.
L996: ## QT
L997: QTc data not reported. Data on PR, RR and QRS intervals showed very few subjects with prolongation or shortened intervals and there were no meaningful differences between treatment arms (data not shown).
L998: ## Immunogenicity
L999: In the phase 2 study (1013), 1 patient was positive to anti-drug-antibody (ADA) prior to the first dose of anifrolumab 300 mg and 5 patients were post dose ADA positive in the anifrolumab 300 mg group. Only one patient had persistent positive ADA for &gt; 16 weeks. Similarly, in study 04, only one patient had persistent, treatment-induced ADA and in study 05 there were 3 subjects with persistently positive, treatment-induced ADA. In all phase 3 data, including the long-term extension study, there were similar proportions of subjects with any ADA or persistent ADA in both anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups, indicating little or no specific induction of ADA response to anifrolumab. Overall, based on only a limited number of ADA-positive patients, there was not trend or pattern suggesting an association between the presence of anifrolumab ADA and the occurrence of AEs, SAEs, DAEs or AESIs. A small number of mild infusion reactions occurred in both anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo subjects who were ADA positive (data not shown).
L1000: ## 8.2.5. Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
L1001: The following sections address the potential adverse events of special interest (AESIs) specified in the integrated analysis of safety plan (summarized above in Section 8.2.1, Safety Review Approach). For ease of review, AESIs will be grouped by Organ System.
L1002: ## 8.2.6. Infections
L1003: Infections were identified as a topic of interest in the anifrolumab program due to the immunomodulatory mechanism of action of anifrolumab. Serious non-opportunistic infections, opportunistic infections, herpes zoster, tuberculosis (TB) including latent TB, and influenza were analyzed as individual AESI categories. Overall infections were not a category of AESI but are discussed here as an additional supportive analysis.
L1004: Infections overall during treatment, as assessed by the system-organ-class (SOC) Infections and infestations, were reported in a greater proportion of patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo group (69.7% vs 57.4%) (Table 61). Most infections in both treatment groups were mild or moderate in intensity, were not considered serious, and did not result in discontinuation from treatment. The difference between the overall rates of infection in the 300 mg anifrolumab group versus placebo group was driven by differences in the incidence rates of mild and moderate infections involving the respiratory tract, excluding pneumonia (comparable in both treatment groups), and separately, herpes zoster.
L1005: The incidence of serious infections during the 52-week treatment periods of the three studies submitted for approval was similar between the treatment groups: 24/459 patients (5.2%; EAIR 5.4) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 32/466 patients (6.9%; EAIR 7.3) in the placebo group.

L1009: Limiting 52-week data to phase 3 data (primary safety pool), the incidence of infections during treatment (any AE in the SOC Infections and infestations) was greater in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (71.7%; EAIR 151.8) than the placebo group (57.8%; EAIR 105.4) (data not shown). Similar to the safety pool of all 3 studies submitted to support approval, this difference was
L1010: driven by differences in the incidence rates of mild and moderate infections involving the respiratory tract, excluding pneumonia (comparable in both treatment groups), and, separately, herpes zoster (data not shown).
L1011: In the phase 3 long-term safety data, including the 120-day safety update through 19 March 2020, the EAIRs of patients with any AE during treatment in the SOC of Infections and infestations were 107.6/100 PY in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 91.6/100 PY in the placebo group. In both treatment groups, among patients with an infection AE, most patients' events had a maximum reported intensity of mild or moderate. Overall, up to 19 March 2020, the incidence rates of patients with any infection in the phase III long-term safety data were consistent with the rates reported in the initial BLA filing.
L1012: ## 8.2.7. Non-opportunistic serious infections
L1013: During the 52-week study pool (studies 1013, 04 and 05), the proportions of subjects with a non-opportunistic serious infection were similar in the study arms (Table 62): 4.8% (22/459; EAIR 5.4) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 5.6% (26/466; EAIR 6.6) in the placebo group. In the placebo group, most non-opportunistic infections were of moderate severity, while in the anifrolumab 300 mg group, there were a greater number of severe events (11/459 (2.4%) in anifrolumab 300 mg vs 8/466 (1.7%) in the placebo group. In the anifrolumab 300 mg group, events reported in &gt; 2 patients by PT were pneumonia (1.7%, 8/459) and appendicitis (0.7%, 3/459). In the placebo groups, events reported in &gt; 2 patients by PT were pneumonia (1.9%, 9/466) and gastroenteritis (0.6%, 3/466).

L1020: Consistent with the 52-week data, the phase 3 long-term safety data demonstrated that patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (9.8%; EAIR 4.2) had a similar risk for non-opportunistic serious infections compared with patients in the placebo group (8.2%; EAIR 6.0). In the phase 3 long-term safety data, the most common non-opportunistic serious infection by PT was pneumonia and similar proportions of patients experienced a serious pneumonia event in the anifrolumab 300 group (2.5%, 9 patients) and the placebo group (2.7%, 10 patients). In the anifrolumab 300 mg group, other non-opportunistic serious infections reported in &gt; 2 patients were: herpes zoster (1.7%, 6 patients) and pyelonephritis (0.8%, 3 patients). No other non-opportunistic SAE, by PT, was reported in &gt; 2 patients in either treatment group. In the long-term safety data, non-opportunistic serious infections were reported at rates of 4.2/100 PY in the anifrolumab 300 mg and 6.0/100 PY in the placebo group, with no trend in either treatment group by yearly intervals.
L1021: ## 8.2.8. Opportunistic infections
L1022: During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), one patient in the anifrolumab 300 mg group had a non-serious opportunistic infection with PT mycobacterium avium complex infection after 12 doses of anifrolumab that led to discontinuation of IP. The subject was treated with ethambutol, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, and rifampicin. The event was considered by the investigator to be moderate in intensity and related to study treatment. Two opportunistic
L1023: infections were reported in the placebo group. One patient had a non-serious AE of oropharyngeal candidiasis (the same placebo subject also experienced a post-operative wound infection counted as a treatment emergent serious AESI of non-opportunistic infection). One patient had a SAE of cryptococcal meningitis with an onset date 32 days after the last dose of IP (the same placebo subjects also experienced an SLE flare and vasculitis with digital ischemia that led to discontinuation of IP).
L1024: Two additional opportunistic infections occurred during the phase 3 long-term safety period; one each in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and one in the placebo group. A patient in the anifrolumab 300 mg group experienced an event of non-serious ophthalmic herpes simplex. A patient in the placebo group experienced an event of non-serious respiratory moniliasis (candida albicans).
L1025: ## 8.2.9. Herpes Zoster
L1026: During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group had an increased risk for herpes zoster infection (Table 63). 28 subjects (6.1%; EAIR 6.9) experienced a herpes zoster event in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 6 patients (1.3%; EAIR 1.5) experienced a herpes zoster event in the placebo group.

L1035: Of the 28 patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group with a herpes zoster event during treatment, 19 patients had cases that were moderate or severe in intensity (Table 63). In the anifrolumab 300 mg group, 2 patients had a SAE of herpes zoster and 2 patients discontinued IP due to a herpes zoster AE. In the phase 2 study (1013), one patient in the anifrolumab 300 mg group experienced SAEs of herpes zoster as well as transverse myelitis with positive CSF PCR for varicella-zoster virus (study 1013 narratives). In the placebo group, none of the 6 herpes zoster events were serious or resulted in discontinuation of IP. All herpes zoster cases in both treatment groups resolved; in the anifrolumab 300 mg group 9 cases resolved with sequelae (most commonly post-herpetic neuralgia) and in the placebo group 1 case resolved with sequelae. Most patients received antiviral treatment: all patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 3 of the 6 patients in the placebo group. Approximately half of the events had an onset during the first 12 weeks of treatment.
L1036: In the phase 3 long-term safety data, a greater proportion of patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (42; 11.7%) had a herpes zoster event during treatment than patients in the placebo group (11; 3.0%) (Table 64). Over up to 4 years of treatment, the EAIR was 5.2/100 PY in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 2.2/100 PY in the placebo group, a risk difference of 3.0 (95% CI: 0.9, 5.0) (see Table 3.6.1.1a in Appendix B). Of the 42 events in the anifrolumab 300 mg group, 30 of the events were of moderate to severe intensity. None of the events in the placebo group were serious or resulted in discontinuation of treatment; in the anifrolumab 300 mg group, 8 were serious and 3 resulted in discontinuation of IP. The EAIR of herpes zoster AEs in the anifrolumab 300 mg group was highest in Year 1 of treatment (7.3/100 PY) and decreased in Years 2 through Year 4 (4.1, 3.9, and 2.2 per 100 PY, respectively).

L1042: Two of the three studies submitted to support approval (1013 and 05) included doses of anifrolumab other than 300 mg (see overview discussion in Section 8.2.8 below). For herpes zoster, specifically, there appears to be a dose-related increased in the frequency and severity of herpes zoster events. In the phase 3 study 05, a similar frequency AE of herpes zoster occurred in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm (10/180 subjects; 5.6%) as compared to the anifrolumab 150 mg arm (5/93 subjects; 5.4%). All 5 events in the 150 mg dose arm were mild (1) or moderate (4); in the 300 mg dose arm, 2 of the events were mild, 7 were moderate and 1 was severe (disseminated cutaneous, recovered with sequelae of post-herpetic neuralgia). For the 150 mg dose, herpes zoster events occurred with equal frequency with different study duration, while half of the events in the 300 mg arm occurred within the first 24 weeks of treatment (data from study 05 derived from the CSR, Table 11.3.2.2.5). In the phase 2 study 1013, twice as many AE of herpes zoster occurred in the 1000 mg arm (10/105 subjects; 9.5%) as occurred in the 300 mg arm (5/99 subjects; 5.1%). In the 300 mg arm, herpes zoster events were labeled as grade 1 (1), grade 2 (3), and grade 3 (1); in the 1000 mg arm, herpes zoster events were labeled as grade 1 (1), grade 2 (7), and grade 3 (2). In summary, herpes zoster AE occurred earlier, with greater severity, and with greater frequency in a dose-dependent fashion.
L1043: ## 8.2.10. Tuberculosis (TB) including latent TB
L1044: In the anifrolumab clinical program, there were no cases of active TB reported during any study. To be eligible for the controlled phase II and III studies (studies 1013, 04, and 05), patients were to have no evidence of active TB or latent TB, unless the latent TB was documented to have been appropriately treated or active treatment had been initiated (see Section 5.3.1 of the study 04 and study 05 CSRs and Section 9.3.1 of the study 1013 CSR, in Module 5.3.5.1). The QFT-G, an interferon gamma release assay (IGRA), is a blood test to aid the diagnosis of *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* infection; both latent TB infection and active TB disease. All patients in the controlled phase II and III studies underwent TB testing at screening and at week 52. If a patient had a positive or indeterminate test during screening, the patient had to meet additional criteria (e.g., repeat testing, chest x-ray, etc.) prior to the first administration of IP.
L1045: In the interim between the screening test and the week 52 test, investigators were asked to evaluate patients for signs and symptoms of TB at every visit and complete a TB surveillance questionnaire (studies 04 and 05) or clinical evaluation questionnaire (study 1013) prior to administration of IP (see sample eCRF in Appendix 12.1.2 to the study 04 CSR in Module 5.3.5.1 and Section 5.2.16 of the study 1013 CSP v3, Appendix 16.1.1 of the CSR in Module 5.3.5.1). In
L1046: the phase III studies, a positive IGRA during the study was recorded as an AESI of latent TB, provided the patient did not have any signs and symptoms of active TB.
L1047: During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), the number of AEs reported as latent TB were 6 subjects (1.3%; EAIR 1.3/100 PY) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (of which 4 occurred in the two phase 3 studies) as compared to 3 subjects (0.6%; EAIR 0.7/100 PY) in the placebo group (of which 2 occurred in the two phase 3 studies).
L1048: During the phase 3 long-term safety study, one additional case of latent TB occurred beyond the first year of study in the placebo arm for a total of 3 cases over 4 years (EAIR 0.6/100 PY); 14 additional cases of latent TB occurred beyond the first year of study in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm for a total of 18 cases over 4 years (EAIR 2.1/100 PY; risk difference 1.5, 95% CI 0.3, 2.8).
L1049: ## 8.2.11. Influenza
L1050: During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), the number of AEs reported as influenza were 12 subjects (2.6%; EAIR 2.9/100 PY) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 9 subjects (1.9%; EAIR 2.3/100 PY) in the placebo group.
L1051: During the phase 3 long-term safety study, three additional cases of influenza occurred beyond the first year of study in the placebo arm for a total of 11 cases over 4 years (EAIR 2.1/100 PY); 14 additional cases of influenza occurred beyond the first year of study in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm for a total of 20 cases over 4 years (EAIR 2.4/100 PY; risk difference 0.2, 95% CI -1.6, 1.8).
L1052: ## 8.2.12. Anaphylaxis, hypersensitivity, and infusion-related reactions
L1053: In the placebo-controlled phase II and III studies (studies 1013, 04, and 05), patients did not receive premedication unless they had a prior infusion-related reaction to anifrolumab. If a prior infusion-related reaction was documented, the investigator could elect to administer prophylactically an antihistamine and/or acetaminophen/paracetamol for the comfort and safety of the patient prior to subsequent infusions. Prophylactic use of corticosteroids prior to subsequent infusions was not permitted.
L1054: ## 8.2.13. Anaphylaxis
L1055: There were no cases of anaphylaxis in any patient treated with anifrolumab 300 mg in the primary safety pool, supportive safety pool, or phase III long-term safety data (data not shown). However, in study 05, one patient in the anifrolumab 150 mg group had an anaphylactic reaction during the second dose. In the one reported case of anaphylaxis with anifrolumab - patient [REDACTED] in study D3461C00005 - a 27 year old white female from Argentina experienced a mild pruritic rash after the first dose of study treatment with anifrolumab 150 mg infusion. The patient was prophylactically administered one dose of diphenhydramine prior
L1056: to the second infusion. On the day of Dose 2, during the post-dose flush infusion, the patient developed anaphylactic reaction with dyspnea, wheezing, flushing, and pruritis; was treated with hydrocortisone, diphenhydramine, and epinephrine; and was hospitalized for anaphylactic reaction. Both the initial pruritic rash and anaphylactic reaction were considered to be related to study treatment with anifrolumab. There were no other anaphylaxis events reported in the anifrolumab development program.
L1057: A possible role for galactose-alpha-1,3-galactose (alpha-gal) glycan39 contributing to the potential hypersensitivity and infusion-related reactions was taken into consideration for anifrolumab. Glycosylation analysis of anifrolumab showed that it contains [REDACTED](b)(4)% alpha-gal on the product Fc domain. It is concluded that alpha-gal glycan in anifrolumab has low risk to contribute to these events because of the following considerations (provided by the Applicant in response to an Information Request): 1) anifrolumab does not have any glycosylation sites in the Fab region, and the \alpha-Gal is present in the conserved glycosylation site in the Fc domain, whereas monoclonal antibodies for which pre-formed anti-IgE contribute to anaphylaxis have alpha-gal in both the Fab and Fc domains; 2) none of the anaphylaxis/hypersensitivity cases happened during the 1st infusion, and 3) There was no distinct regional pattern (tick area). Therefore, testing for alpha-gal IgE in the single anaphylaxis case with 150 mg anifrolumab is not needed.
L1058: ## 8.2.14. Hypersensitivity
L1059: During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), there were more AEs categorized as hypersensitivity reactions in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo group: 13 patients (2.8%) and 3 patients (0.6%), respectively (Table 65). In the primary safety pool, all hypersensitivity AEs were mild or moderate in intensity, and all events in the anifrolumab 300 mg group resolved spontaneously or with symptomatic treatment. One patient in the anifrolumab 300 mg group had a hypersensitivity SAE, was treated, and continued with the IP dosing schedule with prophylaxis given for subsequent infusions (data not shown). One patient in the placebo group had a hypersensitivity AE that led to discontinuation of IP. During the 52-week treatment period, all hypersensitivity AEs occurred in the first 24 weeks of treatment (data not shown).

L1068: ## 8.2.15. Infusion-related reactions
L1069: During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), there were more AEs categorized as infusion-related reactions in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo group: 43 patients (9.4%) and 33 patients (7.1%), respectively (Table 66). All infusion-related reaction AEs were mild or moderate in intensity, and there were no serious infusion-related reactions. One patient in the placebo group had an infusion-related reaction that led to discontinuation of IP. During the 52-week treatment period, most infusion-related reaction AEs occurred in the first 24 weeks of treatment (data not shown).

L1074: Data on specific infusion related reaction (IRR) symptoms is provided for the two phase 3 studies, in which a total number of 79 IRR events occurred in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (39 subjects) as compared to 59 IRR events in the placebo group (26 subjects). Among the events in the anifrolumab 300 mg group, the most common symptoms (reported in \ge 5\% of events) were headache (45.6% of events), nausea (22.8% of events), vomiting (7.6% of events), and fatigue (5.1% of events) (data not shown).
L1075: During the phase 3 long-term safety study, 3 additional cases of IRR occurred beyond the first year of study in the placebo arm for a total of 30 cases over 4 years (EAIR 6.3/100 PY); 11 additional cases of IRR occurred beyond the first year of study in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm for a total of 52 cases over 4 years (EAIR 1.9/100 PY).
L1076: ## 8.2.16. Malignancies
L1077: According to the Summary of Clinical Safety, malignancies were identified in the anifrolumab clinical studies based on the MedDRA SMQs listed in Section 1.1.4.3. Across the phase II and III SLE controlled and uncontrolled studies of IV anifrolumab (studies 02, 04, 05, 09, 1013, and 1145), there were 14 malignancies reported in patients who received any dose of anifrolumab (see Listing 1, Appendix 2.7.4.7.3 in Module 5.3.5.3). Only non-melanoma skin cancers (n = 7) and breast cancers (n = 3) were reported in more than 1 patient. Among 6 total malignancies reported in anifrolumab-treated patients in the 52-week phase studies, there were 4 malignancies reported within 180 days of the first exposure to anifrolumab, which may have been pre-existing: 2 cases of squamous cell carcinoma in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (31 days and 51 days after the patient's first dose, respectively), an invasive breast carcinoma in the anifrolumab 150 mg group (113 days after the first dose), and a B-cell lymphoma in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (12 days after the first dose) (data not shown).
L1078: During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), there were a total of 6 subjects (1.3%; EAIR 1.3) with any malignancy in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 3 subjects (0.6%; EAIR 0.7) with any malignancy in the placebo group (Table 67). The risk difference between the anifrolumab 300 mg group and the placebo group was 0.6 (95% CI -0.8, 2.2).

L1086: ## 8.2.17. MACE
L1087: Major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) events were identified in the two phase 3 studies (04 and 05) by an independent committee adjudicating cardiovascular events. As the phase 2 study (1013) did not include an independent cardiovascular adjudication committee, the events included in the pool of all three 52-week studies were identified by a custom MedDRA term list.
L1088: Only four MACE events were recorded. One patient in the anifrolumab 300 mg group had an AE of acute coronary syndrome (study 04). Three patients with MACE events occurred in the placebo group of study 1013—two of the patients had an ischemic stroke and one had a cerebral infarction.
L1089: ## 8.2.18. Vasculitis (non-SLE)
L1090: Vasculitis has been evaluated because of findings of focal arteritis in a non-clinical study. However, non-SLE vasculitis was not observed in any anifrolumab-treated patient in any of the integrated safety pools.
L1091: ## 8.2.19. Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing Safety/Tolerability
L1092: N/A
L1093: ## 8.2.20. Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
L1094: Study 1013 was analyzed for AE by subgroup separately from the two phase 3 studies and is included in the study 1013 CSR. Of the subjects who were \le 45 years old at baseline, the proportion who had at least one TEAE was 85.3% (116/136) in the total anifrolumab group and 76.1% (54/71) in the placebo group. Similarly, of the subjects who were &gt; 45 years old at baseline, the proportion who had at least one TEAE was 85.3% (58/68) in the total anifrolumab group and 80.0% (24/30) in the placebo group (Section 14, Table 14.3.1.31). Of the female subjects, the proportion who had at least one TEAE was 84.5% (163/193) in the total anifrolumab group and 78.3% (72/92) in the placebo group. The number of male subjects who had at least one TEAE at baseline was too low to analyze (Section 14, Table 14.3.1.33).
L1095: Data pooled from the two phase 3 study 52-week studies is presented for subgroup analysis. For baseline demographics, refer to Table 12. Overall, the AE profile was consistent across all pre-defined subgroups, including gender, age (&lt; or \ge 65 years old), race, underlying renal or hepatic function, BMI at baseline (&lt; or \ge 28 kg/m^{2}), baseline disease-related factors (SLEDAI-2K &lt; or \ge 10 points; IFN gene signature high or low), and based on anti-drug antibody (ADA) status. Additional analysis for the effect of geographic region, and subgroups based on concomitant medication use at baseline is also presented.
L1096: ## 1. Gender
L1097: The majority (&gt;90%) of subjects were female. Although based on a limited sample size of males, the safety profile of anifrolumab was generally similar between men and women with no clinically meaningful differences observed in the frequency or pattern of AEs.
L1098: ## 2. Age
L1099: The proportion of patients in the program over the age of 65 was small (3.2%). Based on limited data, the safety profile appears to be similar in patients \ge 65 years compared to those &lt; 65 years of age. No clinically meaningful differences were observed in the frequency or pattern of AEs. Anifrolumab has not been studied in pediatric subjects &lt; 18 years of age.
L1100: ## 3. Race
L1101: Although the primary safety pool study population was predominantly White (65.9% of patients), the AE profile of anifrolumab was generally similar across racial groups for up to 52 weeks of treatment. No clinically meaningful differences were observed in the frequencies or patterns of AEs.
L1102: ## 4. Underlying renal and hepatic function
L1103: Patients with mild and moderate renal impairment were enrolled, but the phase 3 studies did not include patients with severe renal impairment. Most patients had normal kidney function (eGFR \ge 90 \text{ mL/min}/1.73 \text{ m}^2)—65.8% and 67.9% in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and placebo group, respectively. About one third of patients in each group had eGFR values characterized as mild disease (60 to 89 mL/min/1.73 \text{ m}^2). Few (~4% in each group) had eGFR values categorized as moderate disease (30 to 59 mL/min/1.73 \text{ m}^2).
L1104: Anifrolumab clearance is target-mediated and the primary clearance mechanism is neither hepatic nor renal; it binds to the IFNAR receptors, is internalized and subsequently cleared. Based on the population PK analysis, the clearance of anifrolumab in patients with mild, moderate decrease in eGFR was comparable to those with normal eGFR values (see Section 6.9.2.4 of the Population PK Report in Module 5.3.5.3). Similarly, based on the population PK analysis, baseline hepatic function tests (AST, ALT, and total bilirubin) had no clinically relevant impact on the clearance of anifrolumab (see Section 6.9.2.5 of the Population PK Report in Module 5.3.5.3).
L1105: ## 5. Body mass index
L1106: The safety profile by BMI at baseline (&lt; 28, \ge 28 \text{ kg/m}^2) is summarized for the primary safety pool in data displays of AEs by category in Tables 3.8.7.1 and 3.8.7.2, Appendix 2.7.4.7.1 in Module 5.3.5.3. Summaries of most common AEs and SAEs by BMI are presented in Tables 3.8.18, 3.8.27.1, and 3.8.27.2, Appendix 2.7.4.7.1 in Module 5.3.5.3. The safety profile of anifrolumab is generally similar in patients with BMI \ge 28 \text{ kg/m}^2 compared with those &lt; 28 \text{ kg/m}^2 for up to 52 weeks of treatment. No clinically meaningful differences were observed in the frequency or pattern of AEs.
L1107: ## 6. Baseline disease-related factors (SLEDAI-2K and IFN gene signature)
L1108: In the phase 3 52-week safety pool, 71.6% of patients had baseline SLEDAI-2K \ge 10 points. The safety profile of anifrolumab is generally similar in patients with SLEDAI-2K \ge 10 points compared with those &lt; 10 points for up to 52 weeks of treatment. No clinically meaningful differences were observed in the frequency or pattern of AEs.
L1109: Approximately 82.6% of patients in the phase 3 52-week safety pool were IFN gene signature high at screening. The safety profile of anifrolumab is generally similar in patients with IFN gene signature high compared with low for up to 52 weeks of treatment. No clinically meaningful differences were observed in the frequency or pattern of AEs.
L1110: ## 7. ADA status
L1111: There was no apparent trend or pattern suggesting a correlation between the presence of anifrolumab ADA and occurrence of AEs, SAEs, DAEs, or AESIs, including hypersensitivity or anaphylaxis events. However, given that few patients were positive for ADA in the phase 3 52-week safety pool (25 patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 35 patients in the placebo group), no definitive conclusions can be drawn.
L1112: ## 8. Geographic region
L1113: The safety profile by geographic region (Asia Pacific/Europe/Latin America/North America) in the primary safety pool is summarized in data displays of AEs by category in Tables 3.8.9.1 and 3.8.9.2, Appendix 2.7.4 in Module 5.3.5.3. The safety profile of anifrolumab was generally similar in patients across geographic regions in the phase 3 52-week data. No clinically meaningful differences were observed in the frequency or pattern of AEs.
L1114: ## 9. Baseline concomitant medications—steroids and immunosuppressant therapy
L1115: In the phase 3 52-week safety pool, 51.7% of patients were taking a daily dose of oral corticosteroids (OCS) of \ge 10 mg/day, and 48.1% of patients were on other immunosuppressant therapy at baseline. The overall safety profile of anifrolumab was generally similar in subjects across baseline OCS dose and in patients taking or not taking immunosuppressant therapy at baseline.
L1116: General infection risk in individuals with SLE is higher in subjects on steroids and immunosuppressant therapy.40 Subjects with underlying autoimmune disease may be at increased risk for infections. As an example, herpes zoster is more common in individuals with rheumatoid arthritis and SLE.41 In SLE patients, the risk of herpes zoster is higher in patients being treated with steroids and immunosuppressants.42 As the anifrolumab 300 mg group had a significantly higher rate of herpes zoster as compared to the placebo group, it would be helpful to know if individuals in either group who developed herpes zoster were also taking either steroids \ge 10 mg/day or other immunosuppressant therapy. This may provide information regarding a possible additive effect (increased risk) of herpes zoster in subjects prescribed anifrolumab who are also taking OCS or immunosuppressants. The overall risk for herpes zoster for the phase 3 52-week safety data is shown with additional data according to baseline OCS use in Table 68 and with additional data according to baseline immunosuppressant use in Table 69.

L1128: For OCS use, there does not appear to be an increased (additive) risk of herpes zoster since the number of subjects who developed herpes zoster is similar between low and high dose OCS for both the anifrolumab (11 vs 12) and the placebo group (2 vs 3). However, for concomitant immunosuppressant use, there were a higher number of subjects taking any immunosuppressant who developed herpes zoster than subjects not on any immunosuppressant in the anifrolumab group (17 vs 6). This suggests that there may be an increased (additive) risk of herpes zoster in SLE subjects who are treated with both anifrolumab and immunosuppressant therapy. A similar conclusion has been recently presented in a published summary of the safety profile of anifrolumab--the authors found that subgroup analysis of pooled data from studies O4 and O5 did not suggest any clear trends in herpes zoster cases based on demographics, baseline disease characteristics or concomitant medication use. However, a greater number of herpes zoster events occurred in patients receiving anifrolumab together with concomitant immunosuppressants (9.8%, n=17) than in those not receiving immunosuppressants (3.2%, n=6); the risk difference for subjects on immunosuppressants is
L1129: reported as 8.1 (95% CI 2.6, 13.7) comparing those treated with anifrolumab (9.8%, n=17) to those on placebo (1.7%, n=3).43
L1130: For comparison, there appears to be an increased (additive) risk for serious pneumonia when combining anifrolumab with higher dose steroids (5 vs 1) but not for concomitant immunosuppressant use (3 vs 3). These observations are based on small subgroups of patients in the phase 3 studies and should be interpreted with caution. Additional safety data is required to determine how concomitant use of higher dose steroids and other immunosuppressant therapy may increase the risk of herpes zoster and other serious infections in SLE subjects treated with anifrolumab.
L1131: ## 8.2.21. Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
L1132: N/A
L1133: ## 8.2.22. Additional Safety Explorations
L1134: ## Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L1135: Due to the limited species specificity of anifrolumab, there are no animal models to evaluate the quantitative or dose-dependent risk of carcinogenicity associated with anifrolumab administration. The direct carcinogenic risks associated with anifrolumab administration are not known. Administration of murine IFN-\alpha/\beta enhanced antitumor effects in mice that contained transplanted tumors. In IFNAR1- and IFNAR2-deficient mice or mice treated with analogous murine anti- IFNAR1 or IFN-\alpha/\beta antibodies, decreased host defense to tumors was observed. The relevance of these experimental findings in mouse models for malignancy risk in humans is unknown.
L1136: ## Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L1137: Overall, in the anifrolumab SLE clinical program there were 31 patients with one or more pregnancy as of 01 August 2019. Of those 31 patients, 20 patients were randomized to receive anifrolumab. Among the patients who received anifrolumab and reported a pregnancy, no congenital anomalies associated with IP or no drug-associated AEs were observed.
L1138: ## Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L1139: Pediatric studies were deferred until after completion of phase 3 studies in adults.
L1140: 43 Tummala R, Abreu G, Pineda L, et al. Safety profile of anifrolumab in patients with active SLE: an integrated analysis of phase II and III trials. Lupus Science &amp; Medicine 2021;8:e000464. doi:10.1136/lupus-2020-000464
L1141: ## Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
L1142: Doses of up to 1000 mg IV in clinical studies in patients with SLE are associated with an acceptable safety profile. There was no event of overdose reported during clinical development with anifrolumab and, when administered IV, there is low potential for overdose. There is no specific treatment for an overdose with anifrolumab. If overdose occurs, the patient should be treated supportively with appropriate monitoring and symptomatic treatment as necessary.
L1143: There is no evidence for and no anticipation of patient abuse or dependence potential with anifrolumab.
L1144: In study 1013, all patients were to be followed from the last dose of IP (week 48) through the end of the safety follow-up period (week 60). An analysis of those patients did not identify any adverse effects of drug withdrawal after stopping anifrolumab treatment (see study 1013 CSR addendum in Module 5.3.5.1).
L1145: ## Suicidality and depression
L1146: During phase 3 studies 04 and 05 (52-week treatment period), The eight-item Patient Health Questionnaire depression scale (PHQ-8) scores were similar across all treatment groups at baseline with no clinically meaningful changes from baseline to week 52; small and similar decreases in PHQ-8 scores were observed in all treatment arms from baseline to week 52. There were few AEs related to depression as assessed by the MedDRA SMQ depression (excluding suicide and self-injury) with balanced events between the treatment groups (Table 70). Few patients had AEs related to suicidality; no patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg arms; 2 patients in placebo (PTs: suicide attempt and suicidal ideation). There was no evidence of increased risk in suicidal ideation or behavior based on Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS)—5 subjects in the anifrolumab arms vs 10 in placebo arms. Depression (PT) in anifrolumab 300 mg vs placebo was 18 (5.0%)/EAIR 2.0 vs 13 (3.6%) EAIR 2.3.

L1150: ## Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience

L1152: ## Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting

L1154: ## 8.2.24. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L1155: Overall, the safety profile of anifrolumab IV assessed at a dose of 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in the adult SLE population that participated in the three studies submitted to support approval (1013, 04 and 05) appeared to be similar to placebo, with the exception of certain infections and infusion related reactions.
L1156: There were only 2 deaths in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV group during the 52-week study period. No patients randomized to the placebo plus standard of care group died during the 52-week study period. In the entire anifrolumab IV clinical development program, including doses of 150 mg and 1000 mg IV during a 52-week period and ongoing, long-term, phase 3 extension study of 300 mg (up to a total of 3 years), there were 11 deaths in the anifrolumab IV group and 3 deaths in the placebo group. In long-term, placebo controlled assessment of subjects on anifrolumab 300 mg, a total of seven deaths were attributed to infections (4 pneumonia and 3 COVID-19) in anifrolumab-treated subjects as compared to none on placebo. Deaths in the anifrolumab group were more closely associated with infections, particularly pneumonias.
L1157: There were only 6 malignancies in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV group during the 52-week study period and 3 malignancies in the placebo group. Adding data from the ongoing phase 3 long-term extension study of 300 mg, there were 2 additional malignancies in the placebo group for a total of 5, as compared to 6 in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV group.
L1158: Many of the serious adverse events and withdrawals from study treatment in the anifrolumab IV safety database were related to underlying disease activity or infections. The number of subjects with any infection was higher in the anifrolumab IV group (69.7%) than the placebo group (55.4%) with increased rates of Nasopharyngitis (anifrolumab 300 16.3% vs placebo 9.4%); Upper Respiratory Infection (15.5% vs 9.7%); Bronchitis (9.8% vs 4.3%); Herpes Zoster (6.1% vs 1.3%); Influenza (2.6% vs 1.9%); and Latent TB (0.9% vs (0.2%).
L1159: Herpes zoster infections occurred in a higher proportion (6.9%) of anifrolumab IV patients than placebo (1.9%) during the 52-week study period.
L1160: There were no cases of active tuberculosis. An increased rate of latent TB occurred in the anifrolumab arms, in particular during the long-term extension study. The relationship between
L1161: tuberculosis and SLE is unclear, although a high suspicion of TB and planned monitoring for active and latent TB should be considered, in particular in SLE patients from endemic countries and with high cumulative steroid exposure.44
L1162: MACE events were rare and balanced between anifrolumab and placebo arms.
L1163: There were no cases of suicide/self-injury.
L1164: There was one case of anaphylaxis with the anifrolumab 150 mg IV dose and no cases of anaphylaxis with the anifrolumab 300 mg IV dose. There were more infusion related reactions (9.4% vs 7.1%) and hypersensitivity reactions (2.6% vs 0.6%) in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV group, including two cases of angioedema, as compared to the placebo group.
L1165: Limitations associated with the anifrolumab IV safety database include the lack of concomitant IV cyclophosphamide or other biologics including those that target B cells (e.g., rituximab or belimumab), and the lack of patients with severe renal lupus and central nervous system disease. The small numbers of patients available for subgroup analysis of gender (males), age (\ge 65 years of age as well as children \le 18 years of age), and race precludes determination of the product's safety profile in these subgroups.
L1166: As reviewed above, there may be an increased (additive) risk for herpes zoster with the concomitant use of immunosuppressive therapies and anifrolumab. There may also be an increased risk for pneumonia with the concomitant use of steroids and anifrolumab. The data suggests an increased risk of potentially fatal infections associated with anifrolumab use. There is a possible increased risk of nervous system disorders that requires further assessment. Similarly, additional long-term data is required to determine possible associations between anifrolumab and chemistry abnormalities such as increased serum glucose and gamma glutamyl transferase.
L1167: In conclusion, the overall safety profile of anifrolumab is acceptable given the adequate demonstration of benefit in SLE patients.
L1168: ## 8.3. Statistical Issues
L1169: There were two primary statistical issues that arose during this review; missing data and the question of whether the submission provided substantial evidence of efficacy.
L1170: The amount of missing data for the three studies was moderate to high. It was pre-specified
L1171: 44 Balbi GGM, Machado-Ribeiro F, Marques CDL, Signorelli F, Levy RA. The Interplay Between Tuberculosis and Systemic Lupus Erythematosus. Curr Opin Rheumatol 2018 Jul;30(4):395-402.
L1172: that missing week 52 data would be imputed by available week 48 data. Considering these patients as missing, in study 1013, there was approximately 14% missing data at week 24, and 23% at week 52. Similarly, study 05 and 04 had roughly 23% missing data at week 52.
L1173: Use of such a last observation carried forward (LOCF) approach is generally not recommended as the underlying missingness mechanisms are generally not scientifically justified. In particular, LOCF imputation makes the strong assumption that the outcome of a participant does not change after drop out45. Additionally, LOCF is a single imputation approach, which generally results in underestimation of the uncertainty of the estimated treatment effect due to the failure to specifically account for the lack of knowledge of the values that are missing. The approach to handling missing data should (1) properly capture the statistical uncertainty in parameter estimation due to data missingness; and (2) reflect what is considered to be most clinically plausible.
L1174: In order to evaluate the potential impact of all missing data, including those subjects whose data would be imputed based on available week 48 data, we evaluated the potential impact of these patients having different outcomes on the primary endpoint for study 1013 and 04, and additionally on BICLA at week 52 for study 1013 and study 05. These sensitivity analyses showed that the missing data were relatively unlikely to impact the results and conclusions. The results are further supported by the consistent treatment effects in BICLA at week 52 across studies.
L1175: The *Draft Guidance for Industry: Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness for Human Drug and Biological Products*46 describes two possible approaches to demonstrating substantial evidence: (1) two adequate and well-controlled trials or (2) one adequate and well-controlled trial plus confirmatory evidence. Study 04 represents an adequate and well-controlled trial as a large, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, phase 3 study which was successful on its primary and key secondary endpoints. Though the primary endpoint was changed after the results of study 05 were made known, this change was made before study unblinding. After a review of the DSMB meeting minutes, there were no concerns over the integrity of the trial or the impact on the estimated treatment effect due to the change to the primary endpoint.
L1176: Study 1013 was a dose-ranging, phase 2, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial. The primary endpoint of SRI-4 + OCS tapering at week 24 was significant using the pre-specified \alpha=0.10. However, the study did not specify an appropriate multiplicity procedure for the testing of multiple populations and multiple doses, and therefore study 1013 is not considered to be an adequate and well-controlled trial. Nevertheless, the results of BICLA and SRI-4 at week 52, and other exploratory secondary endpoints were nominally significant
L1177: 45 National Research Council. The prevention and treatment of missing data in clinical trials. National Academies Press, 2010.
L1178: 46 https://www.fda.gov/media/133660/download
L1179: and notably consistent with the results of study 04, providing evidence that anifrolumab is effective.
L1180: Additional support for effectiveness comes from study 05. Although study 05 failed on the primary endpoint of SRI-4 at week 52 and as such, efficacy results are considered exploratory, the results of BICLA at week 52, both under the original protocol-specified rules and under the post-hoc set of restricted medication rules as defined in study 04, demonstrated a similar treatment effect of anifrolumab 300mg to the treatment effect in study 04.
L1181: Results on secondary endpoints, though considered exploratory, were also consistent with results observed in study 04 and study 1013. Thus, while there are limitations to the evidence provided by a study which failed to win on its primary endpoint, when considering these results in combination with those observed in study 04 and study 1013, we consider this further evidence of effectiveness.
L1182: ## 8.4. Conclusions and Recommendations
L1183: To support the proposed indication, the Applicant provided the results from three trials, that evaluated anifrolumab IV 300 mg every 4 weeks as well as two additional doses (150 mg every 4 weeks and 1000 mg every 4 weeks) versus placebo in a total of 1,123 adults with active systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) on stable immunosuppressive background medications. These were international, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 52-week trials that evaluated the efficacy, safety, and immunogenicity of anifrolumab 300 mg IV every 4 weeks versus placebo in 925 adult subjects with active SLE receiving standard therapy.
L1184: Study 04 achieved statistically significant and clinically meaningful results using primary endpoints incorporating either SRI-4 or BICLA composite endpoints at week 52. Study 1013 demonstrated nominally significant results in favor of anifrolumab on BICLA and SRI-4 at week 52, and in study 05, there was an observed improved clinical outcome in anifrolumab compared to placebo in the pre-specified and post-hoc analysis for BICLA at week 52. In study 04, a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (47.8%) achieved the 52-week BICLA primary endpoint as compared to the placebo group (31.3%)[risk difference (95% CI): 16.3 (6.3, 26.3)]. In study 1013, a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (53.5%) achieved the 52-week BICLA secondary endpoint as compared to the placebo group (25.7%)[risk difference (95% CI): 28.4 (15.3, 41.5)]. In study 05 post-hoc analysis, a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (47.2%) achieved the 52-week BICLA endpoint as compared to the placebo group (29.9%)[risk difference (95% CI): 17.0 (7.2, 26.8)]; however, for its primary 52-week SRI-4 endpoint, study 05 did not demonstrate statistically significant superiority of anifrolumab IV over placebo. Additional supportive evidence for efficacy is provided by the SRI-4 composite 52-week endpoint achieved in study 04 by a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (55.6%) as compared to the placebo group (37.4%)[risk difference (95% CI):18.2 (8.1, 28.3)] and in study 1013 by a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (62.6%) as compared to the placebo group (40.2%)[risk
L1185: difference (95% CI):24.0 (10.9, 37.2)]. The proposed dose of 300 mg IV every 4 weeks demonstrated consistent benefit both for primary BICLA and SRI-4 endpoints as well as for secondary endpoints of maintaining steroid tapering and reduction in rate of SLE flare. There was also a qualitative improvement with 300 mg anifrolumab vs placebo for reduction in inflammatory joint symptoms and SLE skin disease severity.
L1186: Based on the above considerations, study 04 is considered an adequate and well controlled trial and provides the primary basis of substantial evidence of effectiveness of anifrolumab. The study showed favorable and statistically significant changes on the pre-specified, clinically meaningful primary and secondary endpoints when comparing anifrolumab 300 mg IV versus placebo. Specifically, a favorable and statistically significant BICLA response rate compared to placebo at week 52 was achieved with anifrolumab 300 mg IV.
L1187: These results were consistent with those from study 1013; however, study 1013 is not considered an adequate and well controlled since an appropriate method for controlling multiplicity was not pre-specified. While study 05 did not show statistically significant improvement in the prespecified primary endpoint (SRI-4), study 05 did show nominally significant changes in the key secondary BICLA endpoint in favor of anifrolumab 300 mg IV dose. Also, disease activity measured by the SLE disease activity index (updated in 2000), (SLEDAI-2K), showed a dose-dependent separation in favor of anifrolumab in study 05. In all three trials, additional confirmatory evidence was noted in the analyses of the secondary endpoints, including steroid-use reduction, and improvement in mucocutaneous disease activity. There was also a reduction in annualized flare rate in study 04 and a reduction in arthritis in studies 1013 and 05 when anifrolumab 300 mg IV was compared to placebo, providing additional supportive evidence of efficacy.
L1188: The safety of anifrolumab in the SLE clinical program was consistent with expected increased risk of infection due to immunosuppression, in particular for viral infections in which Type I IFN is a central part of the immune response. While the 52-week incidence of serious infections between placebo and anifrolumab arms in all three trials submitted to support approval was balanced between anifrolumab and placebo arms—anifrolumab 4.8% (22/459) vs placebo 5.6% (26/466)—the proportion of subjects with any infections was higher in subjects treated with anifrolumab 300 mg (69.7%; 320/459) as compared to subjects in the placebo arms (55.4%; EAIR 99.9/100 PY). Herpes zoster infections, in particular, occurred at a higher rate in anifrolumab-treated subjects compared to placebo (6.1% vs 1.3%). In the 52-week controlled study period, fatal infections occurred in 0.4% of patients receiving anifrolumab and 0.2% of patients receiving placebo. In long-term, placebo controlled assessment of subjects on anifrolumab 300 mg beyond 52 weeks, a total of seven deaths were attributed to infections in anifrolumab-treated subjects (four pneumonia and three COVID-19) as compared to none on placebo. Certain infections occurred more frequently in patients treated with anifrolumab, including Nasopharyngitis (anifrolumab 300 16.3% vs placebo 9.4%); Upper Respiratory Infection (15.5% vs 9.7%); Bronchitis (9.8% vs 4.3%); Herpes Zoster (6.1% vs 1.3%); Influenza (2.6% vs 1.9%); and Latent TB (0.9% vs (0.2%)).
L1189: ## BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L1190: There was one case of anaphylaxis with the anifrolumab 150 mg IV dose and no cases of anaphylaxis with the anifrolumab 300 mg or 1000 mg IV doses. There were more infusion related reactions (9.4% vs 7.1%) and hypersensitivity reactions (2.6% vs 0.6%), including 2 cases of angioedema, in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV group as compared to the placebo group.
L1191: In summary, the increased risk for infections, in particular respiratory infections and herpes zoster, the increased risk for potentially fatal outcome from serious infections, and increased risk of hypersensitivity and infusion-related reactions for anifrolumab 300 mg is similar to the only other approved targeted therapy for SLE and represents an acceptable safety profile for this indication.
L1192: Lower SLE disease activity as well as a decrease in the risk for severe SLE flares and a reduction in daily steroid dose may result in less long-term end-organ damage from patients' underlying disease, less treatment with immunosuppressive agents commonly used to treat SLE with their own inherent toxicities, as well as fewer hospitalizations with less loss of absent work days and ultimately less morbidity and mortality associated with this disease.
L1193: In summary, the efficacy and safety data generated from one adequate and well-controlled study—04—as well as confirmatory evidence from studies 1013 and 05, result in a favorable benefit-risk assessment for approval of the 300 mg dosing regimen of anifrolumab IV as add-on treatment for adult patients with active SLE while receiving standard therapy consisting of either one or any combination of oral corticosteroids (OCS), antimalarials and/or immunosuppressants.
L1194: If approved, anifrolumab, a first-in-class biologic with a unique mechanism of action, will provide a new treatment option for patients with SLE.

L1223: ## 11.1. Prescription Drug Labeling
L1224: ## Prescribing information
L1225: Proposed package labeling was included in this submission; however, a line-by-line labeling review is not included since negotiations with the Applicant are on-going. The following is a high-level summary of major changes to the originally submitted Prescribing Information based on review of the data submitted in support of this application:
L1226: - The tradename SAPHNELO® is acceptable. It has been deemed acceptable by both DMETS and the Division.
- The indication under Section 1 Indications and Usage is the following: "SAPHNELO (anifrolumab-fnia) is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with moderate to severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), who are receiving standard therapy [see *Clinical Studies (14)*]." The review team concluded that it is important to include the statement "receiving standard therapy" rather than [REDACTED] (b) (4) to clarify that anifrolumab is an ad-on to background therapy.
- The description of the mechanism of action of anifrolumab is the following: "SAPHNELO is a type I interferon (IFN) receptor antagonist." Anifrolumab is not included in the FDA Established Pharmacological Class (EPC)Term List. The review team has provided the recommended EPC term for SAPHNELO based on its mechanism of action.
- Limitations of Use included under Section 1 Indications and Usage is the following: "The efficacy of SAPHNELO has not been evaluated in patients with severe active lupus nephritis or severe active central nervous system lupus. Use of SAPHNELO is not recommended in these situations." The review team concluded that it is important to include a statement on the limitations of use as the efficacy of anifrolumab in patients with severe active lupus nephritis or severe active CNS lupus is uncertain.
- Section 4 Contraindications was added as follows: "SAPHNELO is contraindicated in patients with a history of anaphylaxis [REDACTED] (b) (4) with anifrolumab-fnia [see *Warnings and Precautions (5.2)*]." The review team concluded that it is appropriate to include a statement on a contraindication in patients with known hypersensitivity since severe hypersensitivity reactions have been observed with anifrolumab.
- Section 5 Warnings and Precautions—subsection 5.1 Serious Infections was added and included the following language:
L1227: “Serious and sometimes fatal infections have occurred in patients receiving immunosuppressive agents, including SAPHNELO.
L1228: (b) (4)
L1229: Overall, the incidence of serious infections in controlled trials was similar in patients receiving SAPHNELO compared with placebo, whereas fatal infections occurred more frequently in patients receiving SAPHNELO [see *Adverse Reactions (6.1)*].
L1230: Consider the benefit and risk of administering SAPHNELO in patients with a chronic infection, a history of recurrent infections, or known risk factors for infection. Avoid initiating treatment with SAPHNELO in patients with any clinically significant active infection until the infection resolves or is adequately treated. Instruct patients to seek medical advice if signs or symptoms of clinically significant infection occur. If a patient develops an infection, or is not responding to standard anti-infective therapy, monitor the patient closely and consider interrupting SAPHNELO therapy until the infection resolves.”
L1231: In addition to communicating that anifrolumab is associated with overall increased risk of infections, the review team concluded that it was important to provide information to healthcare providers regarding the increased risk of potentially fatal infections associated with anifrolumab based on observations in both the 52-week dataset and Phase III long-term extension dataset as detailed in Section 6.

L1260: ## **"Hypersensitivity Reactions/Anaphylaxis**
L1261: Inform patients that serious hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylaxis, have been reported in patients who received SAPHNELO. Instruct patients to immediately tell their healthcare provider or go to the emergency department of their nearest hospital, if they experience symptoms of an allergic reaction (e.g., anaphylaxis) during or after the administration of SAPHNELO [see Warnings and Precautions (5.1)]. Symptoms may include swelling of the face, tongue, or mouth, breathing difficulties, and/or fainting, dizziness, feeling lightheaded (due to a drop in blood pressure).
L1262: ## **Serious Infections**
L1263: Inform patients that SAPHNELO may decrease their ability to fight infections, and that serious infections, including fatal ones, occurred in patients receiving SAPHNELO in clinical trials. Also inform that they are at increased risk of respiratory infections and herpes zoster during treatment with SAPHNELO [see Warnings and Precautions (5.2)]. Advise patients to contact their healthcare provider if they develop any symptoms of an infection, including fever or flu-like symptoms; muscle aches; cough; shortness of breath; burning when they urinate or urinating more often than usual; diarrhea or stomach pain; shingles (a red skin rash that can cause pain and burning).
L1264: ## **Immunizations**
L1265: Inform patients that they should not receive live or live-attenuated vaccines while receiving SAPHNELO. Advise patients to discuss with their healthcare provider before seeking immunizations on their own [see Warnings and Precautions (5.3)].
L1266: ## 12. Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
L1267: Given the favorable safety profile of this drug, there are no additional risk management strategies required beyond the labeling mentioned. Therefore, the subsequent subsections are
L1268: BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
L1269: not applicable for this review and have been omitted.
L1270: ## 13. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
L1271: The review team considered the following issues to determine which post-marketing requirements should be addressed by the Applicant:
L1272: - Significant proportion of patients who may receive anifrolumab are women of childbearing potential and it is expected that pregnancies may occur in women who use Saphnelo. The pre-approval safety database for Saphnelo was too limited to draw any conclusions about the effect of Saphnelo on obstetrical, maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes. FDA requests an observational prospective pregnancy registry to examine the pregnancy and birth outcomes with Saphnelo use. The goal of this PMR registry study is to evaluate the effects of Saphnelo on obstetrical, maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes. This is a registry-based, prospective observational exposure cohort study and will compare Saphnelo-exposed pregnancy to a disease-matched Saphnelo unexposed pregnancy cohort. Pregnant women 18 years of age at the time of enrollment, with or without Saphnelo exposure will be enrolled. The registry will identify and record major and minor congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, elective terminations, small for gestational age, and any other adverse pregnancy outcomes. We request a prospective patient registry, complimentary to an electronic claims-based retrospective cohort study. The pregnancy registry ensures that exposed pregnant women are identified with data collection on baseline risk factors and safety outcomes prospectively identified with medical chart adjudication.
- It is expected that pregnancies in women who use Saphnelo, target premenopausal women. The pre-approval safety database for Saphnelo was too limited to draw any conclusions about the effect of Saphnelo on pregnancies, maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes. The goal of this observational study is to evaluate the effects of Saphnelo on obstetrical, maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes. This study will use claims or electronic medical record data to assess major congenital malformations, early pregnancy loss, placenta previa, preeclampsia, premature delivery, and small for gestational age in women exposed to Saphnelo during pregnancy compared to an unexposed pregnancy control population. The observational study will assess the obstetrical, maternal, fetal and neonatal outcomes in Saphnelo-exposed pregnancies compared to an unexposed pregnant control population. This study will complement the prospective pregnancy registry study.
- There are no data on the presence of anifrolumab in human milk, the effects on the breastfed infant, or the effects on milk production. The lack of clinical data during lactation precludes a clear determination of the risk of anifrolumab to an infant during lactation. The Division of Pediatric and Maternal Health determined that a PMR for a milk only lactation study is in order because the drug is an NME and use of Saphnelo (anifrolumab) is anticipated in females of reproductive potential; therefore, additional data are required to evaluate the quantity of drug, if any, that is transferred into human breastmilk.
- See section 10 for explanation of PREA PMR pediatric trial.
L1273: Thus, the post-marketing requirements agreed on with the Applicant include:
L1274: - Conduct a prospective pregnancy registry to evaluate the effects of Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) on pregnancy and maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes. This pregnancy registry study may be conducted as part of a multiple-product or disease-based pregnancy registry.
- Conduct a retrospective cohort study in a claims-based database to evaluate the effects of Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) on pregnancy-related outcomes.
- Perform a lactation study, milk only, in lactating women who have received Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) to assess concentrations of anifrolumab-fnia in breast milk using a validated assay. A mother-infant pair study may be required in the future depending on the results of this milk-only study.
- Conduct a study to evaluate the safety, efficacy, and pharmacokinetics of anifrolumab-fnia plus background standard therapy in pediatric subjects ages 5 years to 17 years of age with active systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE).
L1275: There is a postmarketing commitment not subject to the reporting requirements under section 506B: Implement [REDACTED] (b)(4) of anifrolumab drug product and update module 3.2.P.3.4 of the BLA with [REDACTED] (b)(4) as a Critical Process Parameter.
L1276: ## 14. Division Director (Clinical) Comments
L1277: The Applicant, AstraZeneca, has submitted adequate data to support approval of anifrolumab, a type I interferon (IFN) receptor antagonist monoclonal antibody, 300 mg/2 mL (150 mg/mL) in a single-dose vial for intravenous infusion over a 30 minute period every 4 weeks, for the treatment of adult patients with moderate to severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), who are receiving standard therapy. SLE is a serious and potentially life-threatening systemic autoimmune disease with significant morbidity and mortality as well as impact patients' function and quality of life. The toxicities associated with both the approved and the off-label SLE treatments contribute to the long-term morbidity and mortality observed in patients with SLE, highlighting the high unmet medical need in this disease.
L1278: The application raised important regulatory and scientific questions that were adequately addressed by the review team, as detailed in this document, which I will only summarize in my comments. Specifically, after the successful proof of concept, dose ranging phase 2 study (1013), the first of the phase 3 studies (05) did not meet its primary endpoint, SRI-4 at week 52. However, based on analyses of data from these two studies, the Applicant proposed a revised primary endpoint from SRI-4 to BICLA at week 52, of the second still blinded phase 3 study (04). Study 04 met its primary endpoint of BICLA at week 52, along with statistically positive results on the SRI-4 endpoint, ranked now a key secondary endpoint. Of note, both SRI and BICLA are complex composite instruments that capture similar relevant clinical concepts and domains of the disease and are both considered clinically meaningful endpoints for evaluating a treatment effect on clinical response/reduction in disease activity in SLE.47,48 Given the complex clinical development and changes to the primary endpoint for one of the phase 3 studies, the review team carefully considered the data supporting substantial evidence of effectiveness. I agree with the team's assessment and conclusions that the substantial evidence of effectiveness is provided by study 04 with confirmatory evidence from the other two studies (1013 and 05) and supportive mechanistic rationale. Study 04 which represents an adequate and well-controlled trial showing favorable and statistically significant changes on the pre-specified, clinically meaningful primary and secondary endpoints when comparing anifrolumab 300 mg IV versus placebo. Because the phase 2 trial (1013) was designed only as a proof of concept and dose-ranging study with inadequate multiplicity control and study 05, the first phase 3 trial, failed on its primary endpoint, these cannot be considered additional successful adequate and well-controlled trials. However, the review team concluded, and I agree, that when these trials are considered in combination, the consistent results observed in studies 1013 and 05 do provide confirmatory evidence that support the effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV in adults with moderate to severe SLE, a disease with high unmet need.
L1279: 47 Guidance for Industry: Systemic Lupus Erythematosus—Developing Medical Products for Treatment (June 2010); https://www.fda.gov/media/71150/download
L1280: 48 Salmon J, Niewold T, A Successful Trial for Lupus - How Good Is Good Enough?, N Engl J Med. 2020 Jan 16;382(3):287-288
L1281: Across the three studies, there are three randomized comparisons, each with adequate duration (52 weeks), that support the claim that anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher BICLA response rates compared to placebo. Further, two of the three studies provide support that anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher SRI-4 response rates compared to placebo at week 52. The review team also provided a plausible explanation of the discordant SRI-4 and BICLA results in study 05, as detailed in the document. In all three studies, additional confirmatory evidence was noted in the analyses of the secondary endpoints, including a greater proportion of subjects on anifrolumab than placebo able to reduce oral corticosteroid use and a lower proportion of subjects on anifrolumab experiencing a flare of SLE disease activity, further supporting the effectiveness of anifrolumab. For transparency, these results, including the results from the failed study 05 should be described and appropriately annotated in the product labeling.
L1282: The primary safety assessment of anifrolumab was informed by a blinded controlled safety comparison from the three studies (1013, 05, and 04) in 459 subjects treated with anifrolumab 300 IV dosing regimen compared with 466 on placebo for 52 weeks. Controlled long-term safety assessments beyond 52 weeks were also submitted for patients who rolled over from the phase 3 studies 04 and 05, even though this assessment is still ongoing. Overall, the safety profile of anifrolumab 300 mg IV every 4 weeks appeared similar to placebo, with the exception of certain infections (including herpes zoster) and infusion related reactions (including two cases of angioedema). In that long-term safety comparisons dataset, herpes zoster and other viral infections continued to occur at a higher rate in patients treated with anifrolumab 300 mg, and fatal respiratory infections occurred only in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm, including pneumonia and COVID-19. Malignancy was an adverse event of special interest because of the potential in rodent models of IFNAR1 blockade, increased carcinogenic potential has been observed. In the anifrolumab controlled safety database malignancies, excluding non-melanoma skin cancer, were balanced. Safety information was also assessed for 150 mg and 1000 mg dosing regimens used in studies 05 and 1013, respectively. One case of anaphylaxis was reported in a subject receiving the 150 mg dose, warranting a contraindication in patients with a history of anaphylaxis with anifrolumab. The potential safety risks can be adequately managed via appropriate product labeling.
L1283: In summary, the overall benefit risk profile is favorable, and the Division recommended action for this application is Approval, with appropriate labeling. This recommendation also considers the discussion and input from the FDA Medical Policy and Program Review Council on January 22, 2021.
L1284: Post-marketing required studies will include a pregnancy registry, additional pregnancy study, a lactation study, and a PREA study in pediatric patients with SLE 5 years of age and older. The OPQ review team also recommended a post-marketing commitment for the implementation of
L1285: (b) (4) anifrolumab drug product.
L1286: If approved, anifrolumab, a first-in-class biologic with a unique mechanism of action, will provide a new treatment option for patients with SLE.
L1287: ## 15. Office Director (Signatory authority) Comments
L1288: I concur with the recommendation of the Division of Rheumatology and Transplant Medicine to approve anifrolumab-fnia for the treatment of adults with moderate to severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) who are receiving standard therapy. Anifrolumab is a first-in-class type I interferon (IFN) receptor antagonist and has been shown to neutralize the type I IFN gene signature in patients with a high baseline gene signature. Approval of anifrolumab will provide another therapeutic option for patients with this serious and life-threatening disease. The recommended dose is 300 mg, administered IV every 4 weeks.
L1289: The efficacy of anifrolumab was evaluated in three 52-week treatment period, multicenter, randomized placebo-controlled trials. Taken together, these studies demonstrate that anifrolumab confers clinically meaningful benefit relative to placebo as assessed by the composite endpoints of SRI-4 and BICLA. Efficacy was maintained throughout the 52 week treatment period. Additionally, evidence for reduction in the use of oral corticosteroids in all three trials, and in the reduction of SLE flares in one trial was also provided. The efficacy of anifrolumab has not been evaluated in patients with severe active lupus nephritis or in severe active central nervous system lupus.
L1290: The safety of anifrolumab was assessed in the three trials over the 52-week treatment period and compared to placebo. Overall, the safety profile of anifrolumab was similar to that of placebo, with the exception of certain infections, notably herpes zoster, and infusion-related reactions. Product labeling will adequately convey the drug's risks.
L1291: Postmarketing studies will be required to assess the safety and pharmacokinetics of anifrolumab when used with standard therapy in pediatric SLE patients ages 5 to 17 years, to assess adverse pregnancy outcomes and adverse effects on exposed infants, and to measure the presence of the drug in human breast milk.

L1305: ## 16.3. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L1306: ## 16.3.1. Excipients
L1307: The anifrolumab drug product is provided as a sterile liquid formulation (300 mg anifrolumab per vial, nominal, in 2.0 mL) intended for IV administration. The dosing regimen consists of one 300 mg anifrolumab IV infusion Q4W. The composition of the anifrolumab drug product includes 150 mg/ml anifrolumab, (b) (4) mM L-histidine, (b) (4) mM L-histidine hydrochloride monohydrate, (b) (4) mM L-lysine (b) (4) hydrochloride, (b) (4) mM trehalose dihydrate, and (b) (4)% polysorbate 80, and approximately (b) (4) M water for injection. All excipients meet the quality standards from JP (Japanese Pharmacopeia), NF (National Formulary), Ph. Eur. (European Pharmacopoeia) and/or USP (United States Pharmacopeia). There are no novel excipients for the IV route of administration and there are no nonclinical safety concerns with the levels of these excipients.

L1331: ## 16.4.2. Population Pharmacokinetics
L1332: The population PK model was supported by 6049 anifrolumab concentrations from 670 subjects (664 SLE patients and 6 healthy volunteers) from studies 1013, 02, 04, 05 and the IV arm of 06.
L1333: ## 16.4.2.1. Structural Model
L1334: The pharmacokinetics (PK) of anifrolumab was described by a 2-compartment model with parallel first-order elimination and target mediated drug disposition with Quasi-steady-state (QSS) approximation (Dua et al 2015), where the binding rate of anifrolumab to IFN-\alphaR1 is balanced by the sum of the dissociation and internalization of anifrolumab-IFN-\alphaR1 complex. In QSS approximation, k_{on} \cdot Ab \cdot R - (k_{int} + k_{off}) \cdot [Ab \cdot C] = 0; and k_{ss} = (k_{int} + k_{off}) / k_{on}.
L1335: Figure 20. Anifrolumab PK Model
L1336: Ab, anifrolumab concentration in the central compartment; Abp, anifrolumab concentration in the peripheral tissue compartment; Ab-R, anifrolumab-IFN-\alphaR1 complex concentration; CLRES, clearance by the reticuloendothelial system but is simplified as CL hereafter; IFN-\alphaR1, type I IFN receptor subunit 1; k_{int}, internalization rate constant; k_{off}, dissociation rate constant; k_{on}, association rate constant; k_{syn}, k_{deg}, endogenous production and degradation rate constant of IFN-\alphaR1; PK, pharmacokinetic; Q, intercompartmental clearance; R, free receptor concentration; R0, free receptor concentration at baseline. (source: Figure 1 in Population PK Report)
L1337: ## 16.4.2.2. Covariate Effect
L1338: Demographics, laboratory test results, disease characteristics, SLE-related treatment at baseline were evaluated for their effect on anifrolumab PK, these baseline patients characteristics are summarized in Table 75 to Table 78. Due to the very low prevalence of ADA (6.9% [46/670]), the effect of ADA on anifrolumab CL was not evaluated. A post-hoc analysis was performed; there was no evidence that ADA positive or persistent ADA positive impacted PK.

L1360: (source: Table 8 in Population PK Report)
L1361: Body weight was identified to be the most statistically significant covariate on anifrolumab clearance (CL), followed by type I IFN gene signature test status on CL; IFN gene signature status was found to have a larger drop in objective functional value on linear CL than R0. In addition to IFN gene signature status on CL, baseline body weight on CL was also found to be a statistically significant covariate. Body weight was also found to be a statistically significant covariate on central volume of distribution (V_c).
L1362: To account for the increasing trend in concentrations over time, an empirical sigmoidal time dependent function was added to the linear CL of the final model:
L1363: CL = \theta_{CL} (\times \theta_{CL,IFN \text{ status}} \text{ if type I IFN test low}) \exp\left(\frac{T_{MAX} \cdot time}{T_{C50} + time}\right) \left(\frac{\text{Body weight}}{69.1}\right)^{\theta_{CL,wt}} \eta_{CL}
L1364: The final PopPK model parameter estimates are given in Table 79.

L1392: ## 16.4.2.3. Conclusion
L1393: The Applicant's population pharmacokinetic analysis is replicable and acceptable. No covariate had clinically meaningful impact on the PK characteristics of anifrolumab based on population PK analysis.
L1394: ## 16.4.3. Exposure Response Relationship
L1395: The exposure response (ER) analysis for BICLA, SRI(4), annualized flare rate, and herpes zoster incidence rate in the two Phase 3 studies (04 and 05) using ITT population, completers, and completers with high baseline type 1 IFN gene signature were used to support the dose selection along with the Phase 2 dose ranging study 1013.
L1396: To evaluate the exposure-response relationship of anifrolumab for efficacy, the pooled phase 3 (studies 04 and 05) population was analyzed using the full exposure-response analysis set. All patients in the full exposure-response analysis set are also referred to as 'all-comers' (or intent-to-treat, ITT) in subsequent sections. Patients who discontinued from treatment were considered non-responders for BICLA, SRI(4), OCS reduction, and CLASI. Thus, in order to examine the exposure-response relationship in the absence of confounding effect with discontinuation, additional analyses of BICLA and SRI(4) were performed in the subgroup of patients who completed treatment for 1) all-comers, and 2) patients who were type I IFN gene signature test high.
L1397: Based on the treatment received and anifrolumab exposure quartiles (Q1 to Q4), patients were divided into 6 groups: placebo, 150 mg, 300 mg Q1, 300 mg Q2, 300 mg Q3, and 300 mg Q4. The reviewer's assessment of exposure response relationship of different efficacy endpoints (BICLA, SRI(4), OCS reduction, annualized flare rate) and safety events (herpes zoster) were based on the divides mentioned above in different patient subgroups (all-comer/ITT, completers, completers who were type I IFN gen signature test high).
